 Multilevel polynomial partitioning and semialgebraic hypergraphs: regularity, Turán, and Zarankiewicz results Thanks:  Tidor was supported by a Stanford Science Fellowship 
 
 
 Jonathan Tidor
 
 Address:  Department of Mathematics, Stanford University, Stanford, CA 94305, USA
 
 Email address:  jtidor@stanford.edu 
 
  and  
 Hung-Hsun Hans Yu
 
 Address:  Department of Mathematics, Princeton University, Princeton, NJ 08544, USA
 
 Email address:  hansonyu@princeton.edu 
 

 Abstract. 
    
 We prove three main results about semialgebraic hypergraphs. First, we prove an optimal and oblivious regularity lemma. Fox, Pach, and Suk proved that the class of  k k -uniform semialgebraic hypergraphs satisfies a very strong regularity lemma where the vertex set can be partitioned into  poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)  parts so that all but an  ε \varepsilon -fraction of  k k -tuples of parts are homogeneous (either complete or empty). Our result improves the number of parts in the partition to  O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) O_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d})  where  d d  is the dimension of the ambient space and  D D  is a measure of the complexity of the hypergraph; additionally, the partition is oblivious to the edge set of the hypergraph. We give examples that show that the dependence on both  ε \varepsilon  and  D D  is optimal. 
    
 From this regularity lemma we deduce the best-known Turán-type result for semialgebraic hypergraphs.
Third, we prove a Zarankiewicz-type result for semialgebraic hypergraphs. Previously Fox, Pach, Sheffer, Suk, and Zahl showed that a  K u , u K_{u,u} -free semialgebraic graph on  N N  vertices has at most  O d , D , u ​ ( N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d,D,u}(N^{2d/(d+1)+o(1)})  edges and Do extended this result to  K u , … , u ( k ) K_{u,\ldots,u}^{(k)} -free semialgebraic hypergraphs. We improve upon both of these results by removing the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1)  in the exponent and making the dependence on  D D  and  u u  explicit and polynomial. 
    
 All three of these results follow from a novel “multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme” that efficiently partitions a point set  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  via low-complexity semialgebraic pieces. We prove this result using the polynomial method over varieties as developed by Walsh which extends the real polynomial partitioning technique of Guth and Katz. 
    
 We give additional applications to the unit distance problem, the Erdős–Hajnal problem for semialgebraic graphs, and property testing of semialgebraic hypergraphs. 
  
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 Semialgebraic graphs and hypergraphs appear ubiquitously in discrete geometry. These are graphs and hypergraphs whose vertex set is a set of points in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  and whose edges are defined by a Boolean combination of polynomial inequalities and equalities. For example, a foundational result in incidence geometry is the Szemerédi–Trotter theorem  [ 53 ]  on the maximum number of incidences between a set of points and lines in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} . Representing a line by the pair  ( m , b ) (m,b)  of slope and  y y -intercept, we know that the incidence of a point  ( x , y ) (x,y)  with this line is given by the polynomial equality  y = m ​ x + b y=mx+b . Thus we see that the point-line incidence problem can be represented by a semialgebraic graph. As another example, one of the most notorious open problems in discrete geometry is the Erdős unit distance problem  [ 20 ] . This problem asks, given  n n  points in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} , what is the maximum number of pairs of points at distance 1? Since the unit distance condition can be written as  ( x − x ′ ) 2 + ( y − y ′ ) 2 = 1 (x-x^{\prime})^{2}+(y-y^{\prime})^{2}=1 , we see that the unit distance problem can also be represented by a semialgebraic graph. 
 
 
 Two parameters are important to measure the complexity of a semialgebraic representation. The first is the dimension  d d  in which the vertex set lies. The second is the total degree, defined to be the sum of the degrees of the polynomials used in the definition of the edge set. In both of the previous examples, the dimension and total degree are both 2. As every hypergraph has a semialgebraic representation either with very large dimension or very large total degree, it will be important to quantify our results in terms of these parameters. Previous works in this area typically studied semialgebraic graphs and hypergraphs of bounded dimension and total degree. In contrast, our methods are able to handle those of slowly growing total degree. 
 
 
 In this paper, we prove three main results about semialgebraic hypergraphs. The first concerns the regularity lemma. 
 
 
 1.1.  Regularity lemmas 

 
 A central tool in extremal graph theory is Szemerédi’s graph regularity lemma  [ 52 ] . This result gives a structural decomposition of an arbitrary graph, partitioning the vertex set into a bounded number of parts so that between almost all pairs of parts the graph behaves quasirandomly. Crucially, the number of parts in the partition does not depend on the number of vertices of the graph, but only on the error parameter  ε \varepsilon . Szemerédi’s regularity lemma is extremely versatile, finding numerous applications in extremal graph theory as well as in additive combinatorics and other fields (see, e.g., the surveys  [ 14 ,  36 ] ). One drawback of the regularity lemma is that the number of parts, though independent of the number of vertices, is quite large. Szemerédi’s original proof gives a tower of twos of height  ε − O ⁡ ( 1 ) \varepsilon^{-O(1)}  and a construction of Gowers shows that this tower-type behavior is necessary  [ 28 ] . 
 
 
 When studying hypergraphs the situation becomes even more complicated. Different hypergraph generalizations of Szemerédi’s regularity lemma have been proved by Gowers, Rödl et al., and Tao  [ 29 ,  45 ,  54 ] . However, in order for these results to be strong enough to prove useful applications (in particular the hypergraph removal lemma) the statement of the result and the type of structure produced is necessarily quite complicated. The quantitative dependence also is worse; for  k k -uniform hypergraphs, the number of parts is  k − 1 k-1  steps up the Ackermann hierarchy and this enormous growth rate is necessary  [ 41 ] . 
 
 
 In the last twenty years, a fruitful line of research has been to develop simpler and more efficient regularity lemmas for restricted families of graphs and hypergraphs. Since in discrete geometry applications one typically applies the regularity lemma to a geometrically defined graph, these restricted regularity lemmas have numerous applications in this field. One of these extensions is to graphs of bounded VC-dimension. Lovász and Szegedy established an “ultra strong regularity lemma” for graphs of bounded VC-dimension  [ 38 ] . In this result, the number of parts is only  ε − O ⁡ ( d 2 ) \varepsilon^{-O(d^{2})}  where  d d  is the VC-dimension and almost all pairs of parts have edge density either less than  ε \varepsilon  or greater than  1 − ε 1-\varepsilon . This result was quantitatively improved by Alon, Fischer, and Newman  [ 1 ]  and extended to hypergraphs by Fox, Pach, and Suk  [ 26 ] . 
 
 
 For semialgebraic hypergraphs of bounded dimension and total degree, even stronger results are known. In 2005, Alon, Pach, Pinchasi, Radoičić, and Sharir first defined semialgebraic graphs to study the crossing patterns of semialgebraic sets  [ 2 ] . An “almost perfect regularity lemma” follows from their results. Here the vertices of a semialgebraic graph are partitioned so that almost all pairs of parts are homogeneous, i.e., either complete or empty. This result was extended to hypergraphs by Fox, Gromov, Lafforgue, Naor, and Pach  [ 23 ] . Both of these results were quantitatively strengthened by Fox, Pach, and Suk  [ 25 ]  who proved a polynomial “almost perfect regularity lemma” where the number of parts in the partition is only  ε − C \varepsilon^{-C}  for some constant  C C  depending on the dimension, uniformity, and total degree of the hypergraph. 
 
 
 We now formally state the definition of a semialgebraic hypergraph. We will restrict our attention to  k k -uniform hypergraphs which are also  k k -partite. This is not a serious restriction, since any hypergraph  H H  can be represented by a  k k -partite hypergraph  H ′ H^{\prime}  whose vertex set consists of  k k  copies of  V ⁡ ( H ) V(H) . 
 
 
 Definition 1.1 . 
 
 A  semialgebraic hypergraph  in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  is a  k k -uniform  k k -partite hypergraph  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  where  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  are each finite subsets of  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  such that the following holds. There are polynomials  g 1 , … , g t ∈ ℝ ⁡ [ x 11 , … , x k ​ d ] g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\in\mathbb{R}[x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd}]  and a function  Φ : { − 1 , 0 , 1 } t → { 0 , 1 } \Phi\colon\{-1,0,1\}^{t}\to\{0,1\}  so that  ( ( x 11 , … , x 1 ​ d ) , … , ( x k ​ 1 , … , x k ​ d ) ) ∈ P 1 × ⋯ × P k ((x_{11},\ldots,x_{1d}),\ldots,(x_{k1},\ldots,x_{kd}))\in P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}  is an edge if and only if 
 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) ) = 1 . \Phi(\sgn(g_{1}(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd})),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd})))=1. 
 
 
 We call  ∑ r = 1 t deg ⁡ g r \sum_{r=1}^{t}\deg g_{r}  the  total degree  of  H H . 1 1 
                 1 
                
                
                
                
                
                
                
              Previous works have used slightly different definitions of the complexity of a hypergraph. For example,  [ 25 ]  gives a semialgebraic hypergraph a complexity  ( t , D ) (t,D)  where  t t  is the number of polynomials used and  D D  is an upper bound on the degree of each polynomial as a function of  x i ​ 1 , … , x i ​ d x_{i1},\ldots,x_{id}  for each  i ∈ [ k ] i\in[k] . Alternatively,  [ 24 ]  defines the description complexity of a semialgebraic hypergraph to be  max ⁡ { t , deg ⁡ g 1 , … , deg ⁡ g t } \max\{t,\deg g_{1},\ldots,\deg g_{t}\} . It is clear that all of these notions are equivalent up to a small loss in the parameters. 
 
 
 
 Definition 1.2 . 
 
 For a  k k -partite  k k -uniform hypergraph  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  we say that partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  of  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  respectively form a  homogeneous partition  with error  ε \varepsilon  if 
 

 
 
 ∑ | π 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ π k | | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | ≤ ε \sum\frac{|\pi_{1}|\cdots|\pi_{k}|}{|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|}\leq\varepsilon 
 
 
 where the sum is over  k k -tuples of parts  ( π 1 , … , π k ) ∈ Π 1 × ⋯ × Π k (\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})\in\Pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\Pi_{k}  that are not homogeneous, i.e., such that  E [ π 1 × ⋯ × π k ] E[\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k}]  is neither complete nor empty. 
 
 
 
 Our first result is an optimal and oblivious regularity lemma for semialgebraic hypergraphs. We reduce the number of parts in the regularity partition to  O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) O_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d}) . In other words, the power of  ε \varepsilon  is reduced from the inexplicit  C = C ⁡ ( d , k , D ) C=C(d,k,D)  to only the dimension  d d . In addition, the dependence on the total degree  D D  is made explicit and polynomial. We give a family of constructions where the number of parts is necessarily  Ω d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) \Omega_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d}) , proving the optimality of this result. Our regularity lemma has a second surprising property which we call obliviousness. Given the vertex set of  H H , the error parameter  ε \varepsilon , and the total degree  D D , our main result provides a regularity partition which is homogeneous with error  ε \varepsilon  for every semialgebraic hypergraph on this vertex set. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.3 . 
 
 Fix  d , k , D ≥ 1 d,k,D\geq 1  and  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . Let  P 1 , … , P k ⊂ ℝ d P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  be finite sets. There exist partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  of  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  respectively of size 
 

 
 
 | Π 1 | , … , | Π k | = O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) . |\Pi_{1}|,\ldots,|\Pi_{k}|=O_{d,k}\left(\left(D/\varepsilon\right)^{d}\right). 
 
 
 These have the property that for any semialgebraic hypergraph  H H  on vertex sets  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  with total degree at most  D D , the partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  give a homogeneous partition of  H H  with error  ε \varepsilon . 
 
 
 
 While this paper was in preparation, Rubin  [ 46 ]  proved a version of this result where the number of parts is bounded by  O d , k , D ​ ( ( 1 / ε ) d + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d,k,D}((1/\varepsilon)^{d+o(1)}) . For a comparison of his techniques and ours, see  Section   1.3 . 
 
 
 A partition is called equitable if every pair of parts differs in size by at most 1. Via standard techniques one can deduce an equitable version of the main theorem with a small loss in the bound. In dimension  d = 1 d=1 , our techniques give an equitable partition with no loss in the bound. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.4 . 
 
 Fix  d , k , D ≥ 1 d,k,D\geq 1  and  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . Let  P 1 , … , P k ⊂ ℝ d P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  be finite sets. There exist equitable partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  of  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  respectively of size 
 

 
 
 | Π 1 | , … , | Π k | = O d , k ​ ( D d ​ ε − ( d + 1 ) ) . |\Pi_{1}|,\ldots,|\Pi_{k}|=O_{d,k}\left(D^{d}\varepsilon^{-(d+1)}\right). 
 
 
 These have the property that for any semialgebraic hypergraph  H H  on vertex sets  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  with total degree at most  D D , the partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  give a homogeneous partition of  H H  with error  ε \varepsilon .
Furthermore, if  d = 1 d=1 , we can instead guarantee that 
 

 
 
 | Π 1 | , … , | Π k | = O k ​ ( D / ε ) . |\Pi_{1}|,\ldots,|\Pi_{k}|=O_{k}\left(D/\varepsilon\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 One straightforward consequence of  Theorem   1.3  is a Turán-type result. This improves upon a recent result of Rubin  [ 47 ]  who proves the same dependence on  ε \varepsilon  without an explicit dependence on  D D . As far as we are aware, this is the first Turán result for semialgebraic graphs or hypergraphs which has an explicit dependence on the total degree. 
 
 
 Corollary 1.5 . 
 
 Let  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  be a semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  and at least  ε ​ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | \varepsilon|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|  edges. Then there exist sets  S 1 ⊆ P 1 , … , S k ⊆ P k S_{1}\subseteq P_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\subseteq P_{k}  such that  S 1 × ⋯ × S k ⊆ E S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{k}\subseteq E  and  | S 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ S k | = Ω d , k ​ ( D − d ⁡ ( k − 1 ) ​ ε d ⁡ ( k − 1 ) + 1 ​ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | ) |S_{1}|\cdots|S_{k}|=\Omega_{d,k}(D^{-d(k-1)}\varepsilon^{d(k-1)+1}|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|) . In fact, for  1 ≤ i ≤ k − 1 1\leq i\leq k-1 , 
 

 
 
 | S i | = Ω d , k ​ ( ( ε / D ) d ​ | P i | ) |S_{i}|=\Omega_{d,k}\left((\varepsilon/D)^{d}|P_{i}|\right) 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 | S k | = Ω ⁡ ( ε ​ | P k | ) . |S_{k}|=\Omega\left(\varepsilon|P_{k}|\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 These results have a number of applications. For example, the regularity lemma implies the existence of very fast property testing algorithms inside the class of semialgebraic hypergraphs. The Turán-type result gives better bounds on the Erdős–Hajnal problem for semialgebraic graphs. Earlier, Alon, Pach, Pinchasi, Radoičić, and Sharir proved that semialgebraic graphs have the Erdős–Hajnal property: every  N N -vertex semialgebraic graph contains a clique or independent set of size  N c N^{c}  for some  c = c ⁡ ( d , D ) > 0 c=c(d,D)>0  only depending on the dimension and total degree. We improve this bound to  N Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ D / d ​ log ⁡ D ) N^{\Omega(\log\log D/d\log D)}  and show that this exponent is tight up to a constant factor. We will discuss these applications further in  Section   6 . In addition to these, a main application is a Zarankiewicz-type result for semialgebraic hypergraphs. 
 
 
 
 1.2.  The Zarankiewicz problem 

 
 Perhaps the most well-known problem in extremal graph theory is the Zarankiewicz problem. This asks for the maximum number of edges in an  N N -vertex  K s , t K_{s,t} -free graph  [ 61 ] . The best-known upper bound is  O t ​ ( N 2 − 1 / s ) O_{t}(N^{2-1/s})  for  s ≤ t s\leq t , proved by Kővári, Sós, and Turán in 1954  [ 37 ] . This bound is conjectured to be tight; despite considerable effort, matching constructions are only known when  s = 2 , 3 s=2,3 , proved by Brown and Erdős–Rényi–Sós  [ 8 ,  19 ]  and when  t t  is much larger than  s s . For small values of  s s , a matching construction that works for  t ≥ ( s − 1 ) ! + 1 t\geq(s-1)!+1  was given by Alon, Kollár, Rónyai, and Szabó  [ 3 ,  35 ] . For larger values of  s s , a more recent construction of Bukh  [ 9 ]  gives a matching lower bound for  t ≥ 9 ( 1 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ s t\geq 9^{(1+o(1))s} . All of these constructions are described as algebraic graphs defined over finite fields. 
 
 
 However, it is known that any such construction must have either large total degree or large dimension. This is because a  K u , u K_{u,u} -free semialgebraic graph (over  ℝ \mathbb{R} ) or algebraic graph (over any field) has at most  O u , d , D ​ ( N 2 − 1 / d ) O_{u,d,D}(N^{2-1/d})  edges. (For  d ≪ u d\ll u  this substantially improves upon the Kővári–Sós–Turán bound.) This result was proved by Fox, Pach, Sheffer, Suk, and Zahl  [ 24 ]  for semialgebraic graphs and recently by Milojević, Sudakov, and Tomon  [ 40 ]  for algebraic graphs over arbitrary fields. Over arbitrary fields this is tight  [ 40 ] , but has been improved over the reals: Fox et al. prove that an  N N -vertex  K u , u K_{u,u} -free semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  has at most  O u , d , D ​ ( N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{u,d,D}(N^{2d/(d+1)+o(1)})  edges  [ 24 ] . Do extended this result to  K u , … , u ( k ) K^{(k)}_{u,\ldots,u} -free semialgebraic hypergraphs  [ 17 ] . 
 
 
 These semialgebraic Zarankiewicz results are quite important in discrete geometry. For example, the point-line incidence problem in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2}  is naturally represented as a semialgebraic graph in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} . Since this point-line incidence graph is  K 2 , 2 K_{2,2} -free, the above result of Fox et al. implies that  N N  points and  N N  lines in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2}  form at most  O ⁡ ( N 4 / 3 ) O(N^{4/3})  incidences, recovering the Szemerédi–Trotter theorem. In higher dimensions the point-hyperplane incidence problem becomes trivial since all  N N  points can be placed on the same line which is contained in all the hyperplanes. To make this problem non-trivial it is standard to study it under the assumption that the incidence graph is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free. For this problem, Apfelbaum and Sharir proved that there are at most  O u , d ​ ( N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) ) O_{u,d}(N^{2d/(d+1)})  incidences  [ 5 ] , improving previous works of Chazelle and Brass–Knauer  [ 12 ,  7 ] . Though there is no matching lower bound in dimensions  d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 , this remains the best-known upper bound on the point-hyperplane incidence problem. 
 
 
 Note in particular, the semialgebraic Zarankiewicz result of Fox et al. matches the Apfelbaum–Sharir point-hyperplane incidence bound up to a  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss. Our techniques imply a Zarankiewicz result for semialgebraic hypergraphs. This improves upon the bounds of Fox et al. and of Do by removing the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss. In addition, we make the dependence on  u u  and the total degree of the hypergraph explicit and polynomial. As far as we are aware, this is the first result on general semialgebraic graphs which removes the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss and the first which makes the dependence on the total degree explicit. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.6 . 
 
 Let  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  be a semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D . Suppose  | P 1 | = ⋯ = | P k | = N |P_{1}|=\cdots=|P_{k}|=N . If  H H  is  K u , … , u ( k ) K^{(k)}_{u,\ldots,u} -free, then the number of edges of  H H  is at most 
 

 
 
 | E | = O d , k ​ ( ∑ s = 1 k u a s ​ D b s ​ N k − s ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) . |E|=O_{d,k}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{k}u^{a_{s}}D^{b_{s}}N^{k-\frac{s}{(s-1)d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 where
 a s = 6 ​ s + ( ( s − 2 ) ​ ( s + 3 ) + 3 ​ ( k − s ) ​ ( s + 2 ) ) ​ ( s − 1 ) ​ d 6 ​ ( ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) a_{s}={\frac{6s+\left((s-2)(s+3)+3(k-s)(s+2)\right)(s-1)d}{6((s-1)d+1)}}  and  b s = ( s − 1 ) ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ d 2 ​ ( ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) b_{s}={\frac{(s-1)(s+2)d}{2((s-1)d+1)}} .
For  k = 2 k=2 , this implies that 
 

 
 
 | E | = O d ​ ( u 2 d + 1 ​ D 2 ​ d d + 1 ​ N 2 ​ d d + 1 ) . |E|=O_{d}\left(u^{\frac{2}{d+1}}D^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}N^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Later in the paper we will state and prove this result when  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  have different sizes. We also remark that this and all previous results can easily be generalized to the case when  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  live in  ℝ d 1 , … , ℝ d k \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\ldots,\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}  for different dimensions  d 1 , … , d k d_{1},\ldots,d_{k} . 
 
 
 In addition to removing the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in all previous applications of Fox et al.’s Zarankiewicz result, we expect this theorem to have a number of new applications due to the explicit dependence on  u u  and  D D . In  Section   6 , we give a couple immediate applications to the unit distance problem and to counting equilateral triangles. 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Proof technique and comparison to previous work 

 
 All of our results are proved via a new polynomial method tool that we call “multilevel polynomial partitioning”. The polynomial method was first introduced by Dvir in his proof of the finite field Kakeya conjecture  [ 18 ] . Guth and Katz further developed the method by introducing a tool called real polynomial partitioning as part of their breakthrough resolution of the Erdős distinct distance problem  [ 31 ] . Real polynomial partitioning and the polynomial method more generally have revolutionized discrete geometry since their introduction fifteen years ago (see, e.g.,  [ 24 ,  33 ,  34 ,  48 ,  49 ,  55 ,  57 ,  59 ,  60 ] ). 
 
 
 Real polynomial partitioning takes a point set  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  and produces a low degree polynomial  f f  such that each connected component of  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f)  contains few points of  P P . This allows one to efficiently divide a discrete geometry problem into subproblems. The main drawback of Guth and Katz’s original method is that there is no control over the points of  P ∩ Z ⁡ ( f ) P\cap Z(f) . This makes it increasingly difficult to apply real polynomial partitioning as the ambient dimension of the problem increases. To overcome this issue a technique called “constant-degree polynomial partitioning” was developed. This technique iteratively applies Guth–Katz polynomial partitioning many times, each time with a polynomial of bounded degree. Though this method has found widespread success (see, e.g.,  [ 17 ,  24 ,  30 ,  49 ] ), the iterative nature of the proof technique means that all applications have a  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in the exponent. For example, this is the technique that Fox–Pach–Sheffer–Suk–Zahl and Do use to prove their semialgebraic Zarankiewicz results. It is a major open problem to remove the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in all applications of constant-degree polynomial partitioning. 
 
 
 One further extension of the polynomial method was recently developed by Walsh  [ 56 ] . Using more input from algebraic geometry, he showed how to more efficiently apply real polynomial partitioning to a set of points  P P  lying in some variety  V V . Though his techniques are very strong they are also quite technical to apply. To date his results have only found two applications: point-hypersurface incidences  [ 56 ]  and point-curve incidences in arbitrary dimensions  [ 57 ] . 
 
 
 In this paper we use Walsh’s methods to prove a general-purpose tool that we call multilevel polynomial partitioning. We give two applications, using it to prove our regularity lemma and our Zarankiewicz result, though we hope that this result can be used to remove the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in many other results proved using constant-degree polynomial partitioning. Given a point set  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , multilevel polynomial partitioning constructs a partition of  P P  by applying Walsh’s polynomial partitioning result at  d d  levels. In more detail, we first find a low degree polynomial  f f  that cuts  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f)  into a number of open cells each with few points from  P P . For each open cell  C C  (i.e., connected component of  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f) ) we add  P ∩ C P\cap C  as a part of our partition. It then remains to partition  P ∩ Z ⁡ ( f ) P\cap Z(f) . To handle these points we use polynomial partitioning again inside the variety  Z ⁡ ( f ) Z(f) . We iterate this a total of  d d  times, reducing the dimension of the variety by one each iteration. To produce an efficient quantitative dependence, it is important that we use Walsh’s polynomial partitioning over varieties as well as keep careful control over the number and complexity of the varieties produced. Our multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme is qualitatively similar to previous work of Matoušek and Patáková  [ 39 ]  though our work has significantly stronger quantitative dependence. 
 
 
 To prove the semialgebraic regularity lemma,  Theorem   1.3 , we apply multilevel polynomial partitioning to the vertex sets  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  with a parameter chosen in terms of  d , k , ε , D d,k,\varepsilon,D . It then remains to prove that this gives a homogeneous partition of any semialgebraic hypergraph  H H  on vertex set  P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k}  with total degree at most  D D . To do this we use an argument that is inspired by recent work of Bukh and Vasileuski on the same-type lemma  [ 11 ] . We view our  k k  partitions of  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  as giving a single partition of  ℝ d ​ k \mathbb{R}^{dk} . Then a  k k -tuple of cells is non-homogeneous only if it is properly crossed by one of the defining polynomials  g 1 , … , g t g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}  of the hypergraph  H H . We bound the number of cells crossed by the zero set of a polynomial using a Milnor–Thom-type result proved by Barone and Basu  [ 6 ] . 
 
 
 While this paper was in preparation, Rubin  [ 46 ]  independently proved a version of  Theorem   1.3  where the number of parts is bounded by  O d , k , D ​ ( ( 1 / ε ) d + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d,k,D}((1/\varepsilon)^{d+o(1)}) . His regularity lemma is also oblivious to the edge set of the hypergraph and has the additional advantage that the partition can be computed by an algorithm that runs in near linear time. However, his result has the disadvantage that the dependence of the number of parts on  D D  is not stated explicitly and is likely quite large, and the bound loses a factor of  ε − o ⁡ ( 1 ) \varepsilon^{-o(1)} . Rubin constructs his partitions via constant-degree polynomial partitioning which leads to the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss. He also proves the correctness of this partition using an analysis in  ℝ d ​ k \mathbb{R}^{dk}  that also is inspired by the recent work of Bukh and Vasileuski  [ 11 ] . 
 
 
 Our Turán result,  Corollary   1.5 , is a straightforward corollary of the semialgebraic regularity lemma. To prove our Zarankiewicz result,  Theorem   1.6 , we combine the Turán result with a second application of multilevel polynomial partitioning. 
 
 
 
 Structure of paper 

 
 We state the multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme in  Section   2  and use it to prove the regularity lemma,  Theorem   1.3 . In  Section   3  we prove the multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme. We then deduce our Turán result,  Corollary   1.5 , in  Section   4  and our Zarankiewicz result,  Theorem   1.6 , in  Section   5 . We conclude with applications, lower bound examples for the regularity lemma, and open problems in  Sections   6 ,  7 , and  8  respectively. 
 
 
 
 Notation 

 
 We use standard graph theory notation. We interchangeably write  A = O ⁡ ( B ) A=O(B)  and  A ≲ B A\lesssim B  to mean that there exists some absolute constant  C C  so that  A ≤ C ​ B A\leq CB . We write  [ k ] [k]  for  { 1 , 2 , … , k } \{1,2,\ldots,k\} . 
 
 
 
 Acknowledgments 

 
 We thank Jacob Fox for numerous enlightening discussions as well as Huy Tuan Pham for helpful conversations about  Section   7 . We thank Mervyn Tong for pointing out an error in an earlier version of the proof of  Theorem   5.3 . 
 
 
 
 
 2.  Multilevel polynomial partitioning 

 
 In this paper we use the word variety to refer to a (not-necessarily irreducible) algebraic variety in  ℂ d \mathbb{C}^{d} . For a variety  V V  we use the typical notions of degree and dimension. We write  V ⁡ ( ℝ ) V(\mathbb{R})  for the real points of  V V , i.e.,  V ⁡ ( ℝ ) = V ∩ ℝ d V(\mathbb{R})=V\cap\mathbb{R}^{d} . Given polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q r Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r} , we write  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q r ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r})  for the variety  { x ∈ ℂ d : Q 1 ​ ( x ) = ⋯ = Q r ​ ( x ) = 0 } \{x\in\mathbb{C}^{d}:Q_{1}(x)=\cdots=Q_{r}(x)=0\} . 
 
 
 Our main result is the following multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme. The input is a finite set of points  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  and a parameter  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 . We produce a partition of  P P  into  O d ​ ( A d ) O_{d}(A^{d})  parts where each part of the partition is contained in a semialgebraic set  C C . Here  C C  is a connected component of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q r ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(f)  where  Q 1 , … , Q r , f Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r},f  are polynomials of degree  O d ​ ( A ) O_{d}(A) . 
 
 
 Theorem 2.1   (Multilevel polynomial partitioning) . 
 
 Let  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  be a finite set of points. Given a parameter  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1  we produce sets 
 

 
 
 𝒱 = 𝒱 0 ⊔ 𝒱 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ 𝒱 d . \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{0}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{d}. 
 
 
 For  0 ≤ i ≤ d 0\leq i\leq d , each element  V ∈ 𝒱 i V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}  is a tuple 
 

 
 
 V = ( Q ( V ) , f V , P V ) ∈ 𝒱 i V=(Q^{(V)},f_{V},P_{V})\in\mathcal{V}_{i} 
 
 
 where 
 
 •  
 
 Q ( V ) = ( Q 1 ( V ) , … , Q d − i ( V ) ) Q^{(V)}=(Q^{(V)}_{1},\ldots,Q^{(V)}_{d-i})  is a  ( d − i ) (d-i) -tuple of complex polynomials each of degree  O d ​ ( A ) O_{d}(A) ; 
 
 •  
 
 f V f_{V}  is a real polynomial of degree  O d ​ ( A ) O_{d}(A)  that vanishes identically on every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) Z(Q^{(V)})  of dimension at least  i + 1 i+1 ; and 
 
 •  
 
 P V ⊆ Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ∩ P P_{V}\subseteq Z(Q^{(V)})\cap P  is a finite set of points. 
 
 
 These satisfy 
 

 
 (1) 
 
 ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 i ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q ( V ) j ≲ d A d − i \sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q^{(V)}_{j}\lesssim_{d}A^{d-i} 
 
 
 for each  0 ≤ i ≤ d 0\leq i\leq d . Additionally 
 

 
 (2) 
 
 ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 | P V | ≲ d | P | . \sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}|P_{V}|\lesssim_{d}|P|. 
 
 
 For each  V ∈ 𝒱 V\in\mathcal{V} , define  𝒞 V \mathcal{C}_{V}  to be the set of connected components of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(f_{V}) . Now define the set of cells as 
 

 
 
 𝒞 = ⨆ V ∈ 𝒱 𝒞 V . \mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{C}_{V}. 
 
 
 For each  C ∈ 𝒞 V C\in\mathcal{C}_{V} , define  P C = C ∩ P V P_{C}=C\cap P_{V} . These have the property that they form a partition 
 

 
 (3) 
 
 P = ⨆ C ∈ 𝒞 P C P=\bigsqcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}P_{C} 
 
 
 and also satisfy 
 

 
 (4) 
 
 | P C | ≲ d | P V | A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i deg ⁡ Q j ( V ) |P_{C}|\lesssim_{d}\frac{|P_{V}|}{A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}} 
 
 
 for each  0 ≤ i ≤ d 0\leq i\leq d , each  V ∈ 𝒱 i V\in\mathcal{V}_{i} , and each  C ∈ 𝒞 V C\in\mathcal{C}_{V} . 
 
 
 
 We defer the proof until the next section. For now we demonstrate how to use multilevel polynomial partitioning by using it to prove  Theorem   1.3 . This requires bounding the number of cells in the partition and bounding the number of non-homogeneous  k k -tuples of cells. We deduce this from a Milnor–Thom-type result proved by Barone and Basu  [ 6 ] . The deduction from Barone and Basu’s work is somewhat technical and proceeds in a similar way to how Walsh deduces  [ 56 , Theorem 1.5] . 
 
 
 We place Barone and Basu’s result in the following form which we find more convenient to work with. For a variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  and a point  x ∈ V x\in V , we write  dim x ℂ ( V ) \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)  for the maximum dimension of an irreducible component of  V V  that contains  x x . 
 
 
 Theorem 2.2   ( [ 6 ] , see also  [ 56 ] ) . 
 
 Given polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − r ∈ ℂ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}] ,
the number of connected components of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})(\mathbb{R})  that contain a point  x x  with  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) ) = r \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}))=r  is at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d deg Q 1 ⋯ deg Q d − r ( max j = 1 , … , d − r deg Q j ) r . \lesssim_{d}\deg Q_{1}\cdots\deg Q_{d-r}\left(\max_{j=1,\ldots,{d-r}}\deg Q_{j}\right)^{r}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Note that by Krull’s theorem, every  x ∈ Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) x\in Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  satisfies  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) ) ≥ r \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}))\geq r  so this result counts connected components whose dimension is as small as possible. The above result follows immediately from  [ 56 , Proposition 7.1]  and  [ 56 , Lemma 7.2] . For completeness, we include this brief deduction in the appendix. 
 
 
 Now we are in a position to bound the number of cells produced by the multilevel polynomial partitioning scheme. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.3 . 
 
 Given  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1  and  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , let  V ∈ 𝒱 i V\in\mathcal{V}_{i}  and  𝒞 V \mathcal{C}_{V}  be as in  Theorem   2.1  for some  0 ≤ i ≤ d 0\leq i\leq d . Then 
 

 
 
 | 𝒞 V | ≲ d A i ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q j ( V ) . |\mathcal{C}_{V}|\lesssim_{d}A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}. 
 
 
 Together with  1 , this implies that  | 𝒞 | ≲ d A d |\mathcal{C}|\lesssim_{d}A^{d} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider a connected component  C C  of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) \ Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R})\backslash Z(f_{V}) . Suppose  C C  is contained in  C ′ C^{\prime} , a connected component of  Z ​ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R}) . If  C ≠ C ′ C\neq C^{\prime} , then  Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(f_{V})  cuts  C ′ C^{\prime}  and so  C C  “touches”  Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(f_{V}) . Thus we are in one of the three following cases: 
 
 (i)  
 
 C C  is a connected component of  Z ​ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R}) ; or 
 
 (ii)  
 
 there exists  ε C > 0 \varepsilon_{C}>0  such that  f V f_{V}  takes all values  ( 0 , ε C ] (0,\varepsilon_{C}]  on  C C ; or 
 
 (iii)  
 
 there exists  ε C > 0 \varepsilon_{C}>0  such that  f V f_{V}  takes all values  [ − ε C , 0 ) [-\varepsilon_{C},0)  on  C C . 
 
 
 Recall that  f V f_{V}  vanishes on every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) Z(Q^{(V)})  of dimension at least  i + 1 i+1 . Thus every  x ∈ C x\in C  satisfies  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ) = i \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(Q^{(V)}))=i . Therefore, by  Theorem   2.2 , the number of connected components  C C  in case (i) above is at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q j ( V ) ( max j deg Q j ( V ) ) i ≲ d A i ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q j ( V ) . \lesssim_{d}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}\left(\max_{j}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}\right)^{i}\lesssim_{d}A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}. 
 
 
 
 
 To deal with the connected components in cases (ii) and (iii), pick  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0  so that  ε < ε C \varepsilon<\varepsilon_{C}  for all such  C C  and such that neither  f V − ε f_{V}-\varepsilon  nor  f V + ε f_{V}+\varepsilon  vanishes identically on any irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) Z(Q^{(V)}) . We can do this since there are only a finite number of  ε C \varepsilon_{C}  and also a finite number of bad  ε \varepsilon  for which  f V ± ε f_{V}\pm\varepsilon  vanishes identically on some irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) Z(Q^{(V)}) . 
 
 
 Then each  C C  in case (ii) contains a connected component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V − ε ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R})\cap Z(f_{V}-\varepsilon) .
Moreover, for any  x ∈ Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) , f V − ε ) x\in Z(Q^{(V)},f_{V}-\varepsilon) , we know that every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) Z(Q^{(V)})  which contains  x x  has dimension  i i . By our choice of  ε \varepsilon  we know that every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) , f V − ε ) Z(Q^{(V)},f_{V}-\varepsilon)  containing  x x  has dimension  i − 1 i-1 . In other words  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) , f V − ε ) ) = i − 1 \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(Q^{(V)},f_{V}-\varepsilon))=i-1 . Therefore, applying  Theorem   2.2  to  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) , f V − ε ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(Q^{(V)},f_{V}-\varepsilon)(\mathbb{R}) , we conclude that the number of connected components  C C  in case (ii) above is at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d deg f V ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q j ( V ) ( max { deg f V , deg Q 1 ( V ) , … , deg Q d − i ( V ) } ) i − 1 ≲ d A i ∏ j = 1 d − i deg Q j ( V ) . \lesssim_{d}\deg f_{V}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}\left(\max\left\{\deg f_{V},\deg Q_{1}^{(V)},\ldots,\deg Q_{d-i}^{(V)}\right\}\right)^{i-1}\lesssim_{d}A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}. 
 
 
 
 
 Applying the same argument to  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) , f V + ε ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(Q^{(V)},f_{V}+\varepsilon)(\mathbb{R})  gives the same bound for case (iii), completing the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 By a similar but more involved argument we can prove the following lemma which is crucial for bounding the number of non-homogeneous  k k -tuples of cells. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.4 . 
 
 Given finite sets  P 1 , … , P k ⊂ ℝ d P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d} , let  𝒱 ( 1 ) , … , 𝒱 ( k ) \mathcal{V}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{V}^{(k)}  be the data produced by applying  Theorem   2.1  with parameter  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 . Consider  V 1 ∈ 𝒱 i 1 ( 1 ) , … , V k ∈ 𝒱 i k ( k ) V_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{i_{1}},\ldots,V_{k}\in\mathcal{V}^{(k)}_{i_{k}}  and a real polynomial  g ∈ ℝ ⁡ [ x 11 , … , x k ​ d ] g\in\mathbb{R}[x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd}] . The number of  k k -tuples of cells  ( C 1 , … , C k ) ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k (C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k}}  such that  sgn ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) \sgn(g(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd}))  is not constant on  C 1 × ⋯ × C k ⊆ ℝ d ​ k C_{1}\times\cdots\times C_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{dk}  is at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d , k deg g ⋅ A i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 ∏ ℓ = 1 k ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg Q j ( V ℓ ) . \lesssim_{d,k}\deg g\cdot A^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  deg ⁡ g ≥ A \deg g\geq A , then using  Lemma   2.3  to bound  | 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k | |\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k}}|  suffices to prove the desired result. Thus we assume that  deg ⁡ g ≤ A \deg g\leq A  from now on. 
 
 
 Define the list of polynomials 
 

 
 
 Q ~ = ⨆ ℓ = 1 k { Q 1 ( V ℓ ) ​ ( x ℓ ​ 1 , … , x ℓ ​ d ) , … , Q d − i ℓ ( V ℓ ) ​ ( x ℓ ​ 1 , … , x ℓ ​ d ) } . \tilde{Q}=\bigsqcup_{\ell=1}^{k}\left\{Q_{1}^{(V_{\ell})}(x_{\ell 1},\ldots,x_{\ell d}),\ldots,Q_{d-i_{\ell}}^{(V_{\ell})}(x_{\ell 1},\ldots,x_{\ell d})\right\}. 
 
 
 Next, define the real polynomial 
 

 
 
 f ~ = ∏ ℓ = 1 k f V ℓ ​ ( x ℓ ​ 1 , … , x ℓ ​ d ) . \tilde{f}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}f_{V_{\ell}}(x_{\ell 1},\ldots,x_{\ell d}). 
 
 
 Note that  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ~ ) = ∏ j = 1 k Z ⁡ ( Q ( V j ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V j ) Z(\tilde{Q})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})=\prod_{j=1}^{k}Z(Q^{(V_{j})})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(f_{V_{j}}) , so the set of connected components of  Z ​ ( Q ~ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ​ ( f ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  is exactly  { C 1 × ⋯ × C k : ( C 1 , … , C k ) ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k } \left\{C_{1}\times\cdots\times C_{k}:(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k}}\right\} . 
 
 
 Since  f V j f_{V_{j}}  vanishes identically on the all irreducible components of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V j ) ) Z(Q^{(V_{j})})  with dimension at least  i j + 1 i_{j}+1 , we see that each point  x ∈ Z ​ ( Q ~ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ​ ( f ~ ) x\in Z(\tilde{Q})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  satisfies  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) ) = i 1 + ⋯ + i k \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(\tilde{Q}))=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k} . 
 
 
 Define  𝒞 + \mathcal{C}_{+}  to be the set of connected components of  Z ​ ( Q ~ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ​ ( f ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  on which  g g  takes both positive values and 0. Similarly define  𝒞 − \mathcal{C}_{-}  as the set of connected components on which  g g  takes both negative values and 0. Since  g : ℝ d ​ k → ℝ g\colon\mathbb{R}^{dk}\to\mathbb{R}  is continuous in the Euclidean topology, it is clear that if  sgn ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) \sgn(g(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd}))  is not constant on a connected component  C C , then  C ∈ 𝒞 + ∪ 𝒞 − C\in\mathcal{C}_{+}\cup\mathcal{C}_{-} . We now bound  | 𝒞 + | |\mathcal{C}_{+}| ; bounding  | 𝒞 − | |\mathcal{C}_{-}|  is symmetric. 
 
 
 Since  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q})  has finitely many irreducible components, there are a finite number of bad  δ ∈ ℝ \delta\in\mathbb{R}  such that  g − δ g-\delta  vanishes identically on an irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q}) . For each  C ∈ 𝒞 + C\in\mathcal{C}_{+} , there exists  δ C > 0 \delta_{C}>0  so that  g g  takes all values  [ 0 , δ C ] [0,\delta_{C}]  on  C C . Now pick  δ > 0 \delta>0  so that  δ < δ C \delta<\delta_{C}  for all  C ∈ 𝒞 + C\in\mathcal{C}_{+}  and so that  g − δ g-\delta  does not vanish identically on any irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q}) . Clearly each  C ∈ 𝒞 + C\in\mathcal{C}_{+}  contains a connected component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta)(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f}) . 
 
 
 Furthermore, since each point  x ∈ Z ​ ( Q ~ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ​ ( f ~ ) x\in Z(\tilde{Q})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  satisfies  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ~ ) ) = i 1 + ⋯ + i k \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(\tilde{Q}))=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k} , by our choice of  δ \delta , we see that  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) ) = i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta))=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1  for all  x ∈ Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ~ ) x\in Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta)(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f}) . 
 
 
 As in the proof of  Lemma   2.3 , each connected component  C C  of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ~ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta)(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  satisfies one of the three following properties: 
 
 (i)  
 
 C C  is a connected component of  Z ​ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta)(\mathbb{R}) ; or 
 
 (ii)  
 
 there exists  ε C > 0 \varepsilon_{C}>0  such that  f ~ \tilde{f}  takes all values  ( 0 , ε C ] (0,\varepsilon_{C}]  on  C C ; or 
 
 (iii)  
 
 there exists  ε C > 0 \varepsilon_{C}>0  such that  f ~ \tilde{f}  takes all values  [ − ε C , 0 ) [-\varepsilon_{C},0)  on  C C . 
 
 
 Pick  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0  such that  ε < ε C \varepsilon<\varepsilon_{C}  for all such  C C  and such that neither  f ~ − ε \tilde{f}-\varepsilon  nor  f ~ + ε \tilde{f}+\varepsilon  vanishes identically on any irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta) . Then we see that each point  x ∈ Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ , f ~ ± ε ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ~ ) x\in Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta,\tilde{f}\pm\varepsilon)(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(\tilde{f})  satisfies  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ , f ~ ± ε ) ) = i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 2 \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta,\tilde{f}\pm\varepsilon))=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-2 . 
 
 
 Thus by  Theorem   2.2 , the number of  C C  in case (i) is bounded by 
 

 
 
 ≲ d , k deg g ∏ ℓ = 1 k ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg Q j ( V ℓ ) ( max ⁡ { deg ⁡ g , deg ⁡ Q 1 ( V 1 ) , … , deg ⁡ Q d − i k ( V k ) } ) i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 ≲ d , k deg g ⋅ A i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 ∏ ℓ = 1 k ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg Q j ( V ℓ ) . \begin{split}\lesssim_{d,k}\deg g\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}&\left(\max\left\{\deg g,\deg Q_{1}^{(V_{1})},\ldots,\deg Q_{d-i_{k}}^{(V_{k})}\right\}\right)^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\\
&\lesssim_{d,k}\deg g\cdot A^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}.\end{split} 
 
 
 
 
 Furthermore, each  C C  in case (ii) above contains a connected component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ , f ~ − ε ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta,\tilde{f}-\varepsilon)(\mathbb{R})  and each  C C  in case (iii) above contains a connected component of  Z ⁡ ( Q ~ , g − δ , f ~ + ε ) ​ ( ℝ ) Z(\tilde{Q},g-\delta,\tilde{f}+\varepsilon)(\mathbb{R}) . Again by  Theorem   2.2 , the number of  C C  in cases (ii) and (iii) is bounded by 
 

 
 
 ≲ d , k deg g ⋅ deg f ~ ∏ ℓ = 1 k ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg Q j ( V ℓ ) ( max ⁡ { deg ⁡ g , deg ⁡ f ~ , deg ⁡ Q 1 ( V 1 ) , … , deg ⁡ Q d − i k ( V k ) } ) i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 2 ≲ d , k deg g ⋅ A i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 ∏ ℓ = 1 k ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg Q j ( V ℓ ) . \begin{split}\lesssim_{d,k}\deg g\cdot\deg\tilde{f}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}&\left(\max\left\{\deg g,\deg\tilde{f},\deg Q_{1}^{(V_{1})},\ldots,\deg Q_{d-i_{k}}^{(V_{k})}\right\}\right)^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-2}\\
&\lesssim_{d,k}\deg g\cdot A^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}.\end{split} 
 
 
 
 
 Summing these three cases gives the desired bound on  | 𝒞 + | |\mathcal{C}_{+}| . An identical argument bounds  | 𝒞 − | |\mathcal{C}_{-}| .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   1.3 . 
 
 For some large constant  C d , k C_{d,k} , define  A = C d , k ​ D / ε ≥ 1 A=C_{d,k}D/\varepsilon\geq 1 .  for each  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k 1\leq\ell\leq k .

 By  4 , the fraction of  k k -tuples of vertices contributed by each  k k -tuple of cells  ( C 1 , … , C k ) ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k (C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k}}  is
