∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of  Corollary   1.4 . 
 
 The deduction of the  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  case of  Corollary   1.4  from  Theorem   1.3  is standard (see, e.g.,  [ 25 , Proof of Theorem 1.3] ). We include a proof for completeness. 
 
 
 Let  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  be the partitions of  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}  produced by  Theorem   1.3  with parameters  d , k , D ≥ 1 d,k,D\geq 1  and  ε / 2 \varepsilon/2 . For each  1 ≤ i ≤ k 1\leq i\leq k , say say  N i = | P i | N_{i}=|P_{i}|  and define  K i = ⌈ 4 ​ k ​ ε − 1 ​ | Π i | ⌉ K_{i}=\left\lceil 4k\varepsilon^{-1}|\Pi_{i}|\right\rceil . Index  P i = { p 1 , … , p N i } P_{i}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{N_{i}}\}  so that each part  π ∈ Π i \pi\in\Pi_{i}  is an interval in this indexing. Now define  Π i ′ \Pi_{i}^{\prime}  to be an equitable partition of  P i P_{i}  into  K i K_{i}  pieces, each of which is an interval of length  ⌊ N i / K i ⌋ \left\lfloor N_{i}/K_{i}\right\rfloor  or  ⌈ N i / K i ⌉ \left\lceil N_{i}/K_{i}\right\rceil . We claim that  Π 1 ′ , … , Π k ′ \Pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Pi_{k}^{\prime}  have the desired properties. 
 
 
 Call a part  π ∈ Π i ′ \pi\in\Pi_{i}^{\prime}  bad if it is not contained in a part of  Π i \Pi_{i} . Note that the number of bad parts is at most  | Π i | |\Pi_{i}|  and each bad part has size at most  2 ​ N i / K i 2N_{i}/K_{i} . For a semialgebraic hypergraph  H H  on  P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k} , a  k k -tuple of vertices  ( x 1 , … , x k ) (x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})  is contained in a  k k -tuple of parts of  ( π 1 , … , π k ) ∈ Π 1 ′ × ⋯ × Π k ′ (\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})\in\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times\Pi_{k}^{\prime}  that is not homogeneous only if either it is contained in a  k k -tuple of parts of  Π 1 × ⋯ × Π k \Pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\Pi_{k}  that is not homogeneous or one of the  π i \pi_{i}  is bad. The number of  k k -tuples of vertices of the first type is at most  ( ε / 2 ) N 1 ⋯ N k (\varepsilon/2)N_{1}\cdots N_{k} . The number of  k k -tuples of vertices of the second type is at most 
 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 k ( ∏ j ≠ i N j ) 2 ​ N i K i | Π i | = N 1 ⋯ N k ∑ i = 1 k 2 ​ | Π i | K i ≤ ε 2 N 1 ⋯ N k . \sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\prod_{j\neq i}N_{j}\right)\frac{2N_{i}}{K_{i}}|\Pi_{i}|=N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{2|\Pi_{i}|}{K_{i}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}N_{1}\cdots N_{k}. 
 
 
 
 
 Therefore  Π 1 ′ , … , Π k ′ \Pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Pi_{k}^{\prime}  form a homogeneous partition of  H H  with error  ε \varepsilon . Note that 
 

 
 
 | Π i ′ | = K i = ⌈ 4 ​ k ​ ε − 1 ​ | Π i | ⌉ = O d , k ​ ( D d ​ ε − ( d + 1 ) ) . |\Pi_{i}^{\prime}|=K_{i}=\left\lceil 4k\varepsilon^{-1}|\Pi_{i}|\right\rceil=O_{d,k}(D^{d}\varepsilon^{-(d+1)}). 
 
 
 
 
 Now we show how to save a factor of  ε \varepsilon  when  d = 1 d=1 . For  P i ⊂ ℝ P_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}  and any positive integer  A A , we can find  y 1 , … , y A − 1 y_{1},\ldots,y_{A-1}  so that 
 

 
 
 P i ∩ ( − ∞ , y 1 ) , P i ∩ ( y 1 , y 2 ) , … , P i ∩ ( y A − 2 , y A − 1 ) , P i ∩ ( y A − 1 , + ∞ ) P_{i}\cap(-\infty,y_{1}),P_{i}\cap(y_{1},y_{2}),\ldots,P_{i}\cap(y_{A-2},y_{A-1}),P_{i}\cap(y_{A-1},+\infty) 
 
 
 is an equitable partition of  P i P_{i}  into  A A  pieces. Instead of applying  Theorem   2.1 , we will construct the data that the theorem produces by hand. Define  𝒱 0 = ∅ \mathcal{V}_{0}=\emptyset  and  𝒱 1 = { ( Q ( ℂ ) , f ℂ , P ℂ ) } \mathcal{V}_{1}=\left\{(Q^{(\mathbb{C})},f_{\mathbb{C}},P_{\mathbb{C}})\right\}  where  Q ( ℂ ) = ∅ Q^{(\mathbb{C})}=\emptyset , where  P ℂ = P i P_{\mathbb{C}}=P_{i} , and where  f ℂ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 A − 1 ( x − y j ) f_{\mathbb{C}}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{A-1}(x-y_{j}) . One can easily check that these satisfy all of the desired properties and that the partition of  P i P_{i}  produced is the above equitable partition. 
 
 
 Applying this procedure for  i = 1 , … , k i=1,\ldots,k  with  A = C 1 , k ​ D / ε A=C_{1,k}D/\varepsilon , we produce equitable partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  of  P 1 , … , P k P_{1},\ldots,P_{k} . Then the proof of  Theorem   1.3  shows that these partitions have the desired property.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Constructing the partition 

 
 In this section, we explain how to construct the multilevel polynomial partition in  Theorem   2.1 . The key tool is a quantitatively efficient real polynomial partitioning result over irreducible varieties introduced by Walsh  [ 56 ] . This allows us to afford to do real polynomial partitioning with polynomials of super-constant degree and lets us avoid the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in the exponent. These tools were also used in a later paper of Walsh  [ 57 ]  to prove an optimal point-curve incidence bound, though that setting is considerably more technical than this one. 
 
 
 To introduce Walsh’s real polynomial partitioning result, we need to introduce a more refined notion of degree that gives more precise quantitative control over a variety. This will be used extensively throughout the proof. 
 
 
 Definition 3.1   (Partial degrees) . 
 
 For an irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  and  1 ≤ i ≤ d − dim V 1\leq i\leq d-\dim V , define the  i i -th partial degree ,  δ i ​ ( V ) \delta_{i}(V) , to be the minimum positive integer such that there exist polynomials  f 1 , … , f t ∈ ℂ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] f_{1},\ldots,f_{t}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}]  of degree at most  δ i ​ ( V ) \delta_{i}(V)  such that  V ⊆ Z ⁡ ( f 1 , … , f t ) V\subseteq Z(f_{1},\ldots,f_{t})  and such that every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( f 1 , … , f t ) Z(f_{1},\ldots,f_{t})  containing  V V  has dimension at least  d − i d-i . We define  δ 0 ​ ( V ) = 0 \delta_{0}(V)=0  and  δ i ​ ( V ) = ∞ \delta_{i}(V)=\infty  for  i > d − dim V i>d-\dim V . 
 
 
 
 By a short argument,  [ 56 , Lemma 2.3] , these are known to satisfy  δ 1 ​ ( V ) ≤ δ 2 ​ ( V ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ δ d − dim V ​ ( V ) \delta_{1}(V)\leq\delta_{2}(V)\leq\cdots\leq\delta_{d-\dim V}(V) . 
 
 
 To give some intuition for this definition, consider polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − r ∈ ℂ ⁡ [ x 1 , … ​ x d ] Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots x_{d}]  satisfying  deg ⁡ Q 1 ≤ ⋯ ≤ deg ⁡ Q d − r \deg Q_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\deg Q_{d-r} . Suppose that the variety  V = Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) V=Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  is irreducible. If  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 ) , … , Z ⁡ ( Q d − r ) Z(Q_{1}),\ldots,Z(Q_{d-r})  intersect nicely enough, (i.e., if  V V  is a complete intersection) then  V V  will have dimension  r r  and degree  deg Q 1 ⋯ deg Q d − r \deg Q_{1}\cdots\deg Q_{d-r} . Furthermore, in this case  δ i ​ ( V ) = deg ⁡ Q i \delta_{i}(V)=\deg Q_{i}  for all  i i . Although not every irreducible variety is a complete intersection, Walsh’s techniques say that we are always approximately in this situation. In particular, for any irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  of dimension  r r , there exist polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − r ∈ ℂ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}]  so that  V V  is an irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  and so that  δ i ​ ( V ) = deg ⁡ Q i \delta_{i}(V)=\deg Q_{i} . Furthermore,  deg ⁡ V \deg V  and  deg Q 1 ⋯ deg Q d − r \deg Q_{1}\cdots\deg Q_{d-r}  will be equal up to a multiplicative constant that only depends on the ambient dimension. We state the second part of this result below and something a little stronger than the first result as  Proposition   3.5 . 
 
 
 Proposition 3.2   ( [ 56 , Corollaries 5.4 and 5.7] ) . 
 
 For an irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d} , 
 

 
 
 ∏ i = 1 d − dim V δ i ( V ) ≲ d deg V ≤ ∏ i = 1 d − dim V δ i ( V ) . \prod_{i=1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{i}(V)\lesssim_{d}\deg V\leq\prod_{i=1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{i}(V). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Having introduced the definition of partial degrees, we can now state the result of Walsh that allows us to do efficient real polynomial partitioning over any irreducible varieties. The formulation below is slightly different from what is stated in his paper. We include a short deduction in the appendix. 
 
 
 Theorem 3.3   ( [ 56 , Theorem 3.2] ) . 
 
 Let  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  be an irreducible variety and let  P ⊆ V ⁡ ( ℝ ) P\subseteq V(\mathbb{R})  be a finite set of points. For  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 , there exists a polynomial  f ∈ ℝ ⁡ [ x 1 , … , x d ] f\in\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}]  with  deg f ≲ d A \deg f\lesssim_{d}A  so that  f f  does not vanish identically on  V V  and each connected component of  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f)  contains at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d | P | A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i δ j ​ ( V ) \lesssim_{d}\frac{|P|}{A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\delta_{j}(V)} 
 
 
 points of  P P , where  dim V ≤ i ≤ d \dim V\leq i\leq d  is the unique integer with  δ d − i ​ ( V ) ≤ A < δ d − i + 1 ​ ( V ) \delta_{d-i}(V)\leq A<\delta_{d-i+1}(V) . 
 
 
 
 As mentioned above, we can approximate any irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  by  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − dim V ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-\dim V})  for some polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − dim V Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-\dim V}  with  deg ⁡ Q i = δ i ​ ( V ) \deg Q_{i}=\delta_{i}(V)  (see  [ 56 , Lemma 5.3] ). However, for technical reasons, we will need to make a slightly different choice for the polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − dim V Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-\dim V} . To be more specific, the other irreducible components of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − dim V ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-\dim V})  might have dimensions larger than expected, making it difficult to apply  Theorem   2.2 . Below we make a definition to identify those problematic components and then introduce another result of Walsh that overcomes this issue. 
 
 
 Definition 3.4 . 
 
 For an irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  of dimension  r r  and polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − r Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}  such that  V V  is an irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}) , we define the  envelope   ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) \mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  by 
 

 
 
 ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) = ⋃ i = 1 d − r ℰ V ( i ) ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) \mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{d-r}\mathcal{E}_{V}^{(i)}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}) 
 
 
 where  ℰ V ( i ) ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) \mathcal{E}_{V}^{(i)}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  is the union of the irreducible components of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q i ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{i})  of dimension at least  d − i + 1 d-i+1 . 
 
 
 
 By Krull’s theorem, it is clear that  dim x ℂ ( Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) ) = r \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}))=r  for all  x ∈ Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) ∖ ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) x\in Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})\setminus\mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}) . Therefore to make sure that we are in position to apply  Theorem   2.2 , we should manually excise  ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − r ) \mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r})  via a polynomial of small degree. This was done in  [ 56 , Section 6] . Expanding the definitions used in  [ 56 , Lemma 6.8]  gives the following statement. (Actually it gives something strictly stronger, but we only state the parts that we will use.) 
 
 
 Proposition 3.5   ( [ 56 , Lemma 6.8] ) . 
 
 Given an irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  of dimension  j j , there exist polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − j Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j}  and  F 1 , … , F d − j − 1 F_{1},\ldots,F_{d-j-1}  with the following properties: 
 
 •  
 
 deg Q k ≲ d δ k ( V ) \deg Q_{k}\lesssim_{d}\delta_{k}(V)  for each  1 ≤ k ≤ d − j 1\leq k\leq d-j ; 
 
 •  
 
 deg ⁡ F k < δ k ​ ( V ) \deg F_{k}<\delta_{k}(V)  for each  1 ≤ k ≤ d − j − 1 1\leq k\leq d-j-1 ; 
 
 •  
 
 V ⊆ Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − j ) V\subseteq Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j}) ; 
 
 •  
 
 F k F_{k}  vanishes identically on each irreducible component of  ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − j ) \mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j})  of dimension  d − k d-k  for each  1 ≤ k ≤ d − j − 1 1\leq k\leq d-j-1 ; 
 
 •  
 
 F k F_{k}  does not vanish identically on  V V  for each  1 ≤ k ≤ d − j − 1 1\leq k\leq d-j-1 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 We now prove  Theorem   2.1  using  Theorem   3.3  and  Proposition   3.5 . 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   2.1 . 
 
 We construct the desired data in steps numbered  d , d − 1 , … , 0 d,d-1,\ldots,0 . At step  j j  we start with a set  𝒰 j \mathcal{U}_{j}  whose elements are pairs  ( V , P V ) (V,P_{V})  where  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  is an irreducible  j j -dimensional variety and  P V ⊆ V ∩ P P_{V}\subseteq V\cap P . During this step we will add some elements to  𝒱 j , 𝒱 j + 1 , … , 𝒱 d \mathcal{V}_{j},\mathcal{V}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{d}  and also produce a set  𝒰 j − 1 \mathcal{U}_{j-1} . 
 
 
 To start, define  𝒰 d = { ( ℂ d , P ) } \mathcal{U}_{d}=\{(\mathbb{C}^{d},P)\} . We will ensure that before step  j j , we have the partition 
 

 
 (5) 
 
 P = ⨆ ( V , P V ) ∈ 𝒰 j P V ⊔ ⨆ V ∈ 𝒱 ⨆ C ∈ 𝒞 V P C . P=\bigsqcup_{(V,P_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}P_{V}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\bigsqcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}}P_{C}. 
 
 
 Note that this is satisfied before step  d d . 
 
 
 Suppose we are given  𝒰 j \mathcal{U}_{j}  for some  0 ≤ j ≤ d 0\leq j\leq d  and that  5  is satisfied. Step  j j  proceeds as follows. For each  ( V , P V ) ∈ 𝒰 j (V,P_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{j} , define  j ≤ i ≤ d j\leq i\leq d  so that 
 

 
 
 δ 1 ​ ( V ) ≤ δ 2 ​ ( V ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ δ d − i ​ ( V ) ≤ A < δ d − i + 1 ​ ( V ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ δ d − j ​ ( V ) . \delta_{1}(V)\leq\delta_{2}(V)\leq\cdots\leq\delta_{d-i}(V)\leq A<\delta_{d-i+1}(V)\leq\cdots\leq\delta_{d-j}(V). 
 
 
 We apply  Proposition   3.5  to  V V  to produce polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − j Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j}  and  F 1 , … , F d − j − 1 F_{1},\ldots,F_{d-j-1} . 
 
 
 By real polynomial partitioning,  Theorem   3.3 , there exists a real polynomial  f part f_{\prt}  of degree  O d ​ ( A ) O_{d}(A)  that does not vanish identically on  V V  such that each connected component of  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f part ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f_{\prt})  contains at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d | P V | A i ​ ∏ ℓ = 1 d − i δ ℓ ​ ( V ) \lesssim_{d}\frac{\left\lvert P_{V}\right\rvert}{A^{i}\prod_{\ell=1}^{d-i}\delta_{\ell}(V)} 
 
 
 points of  P V P_{V} . Define  f V = | F 1 F 2 ⋯ F d − i − 1 | 2 f part f_{V}=|F_{1}F_{2}\cdots F_{d-i-1}|^{2}f_{\prt} . We add  V = ( Q ( V ) , f V , P V ) V=(Q^{(V)},f_{V},P_{V})  to  𝒱 i \mathcal{V}_{i}  where  Q ( V ) = ( Q 1 , … , Q d − i ) Q^{(V)}=(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-i}) . Note that this is a  ( d − i ) (d-i) -tuple of polynomials; the last  i − j i-j  polynomials are not included in the data. 
 
 
 By our choice of  i i  and the above properties, we see that each of the polynomials  Q 1 , … , Q d − i , f V Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-i},f_{V}  has degree  O d ​ ( A ) O_{d}(A) . Clearly  f V f_{V}  is a real polynomial. By definition, every irreducible component of  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − i ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-i})  of dimension at least  i + 1 i+1  is contained in an irreducible component of  ℰ V ​ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − j ) \mathcal{E}_{V}(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j})  of dimension at least  i + 1 i+1 , meaning that  f V f_{V}  vanishes identically on it. Finally,  P V ⊆ V ⊆ Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − j ) ⊆ Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q d − i ) P_{V}\subseteq V\subseteq Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-j})\subseteq Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-i}) . This shows that  ( Q ( V ) , f V , P V ) (Q^{(V)},f_{V},P_{V})  has the desired properties. 
 
 
 We define  𝒞 V \mathcal{C}_{V}  to be the set of connected components of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(f_{V})  and for each  C ∈ 𝒞 V C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}  we define  P C = C ∩ P V P_{C}=C\cap P_{V} . Note that  { P C } C ∈ 𝒞 V \{P_{C}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}}  is a partition of  P V ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V ) P_{V}\setminus Z(f_{V}) . 
 
 
 Let  W 1 , … , W s W_{1},\ldots,W_{s}  be the irreducible components of  V ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V ) V\cap Z(f_{V}) . Note that neither  f part f_{\prt}  nor any of  F 1 , … , F d − i − 1 F_{1},\ldots,F_{d-i-1}  vanish identically on  V V , meaning that  f V f_{V}  does not vanish identically on  V V . Thus by Krull’s theorem, each  W k W_{k}  has dimension  j − 1 j-1 . 
 
 
 We define  P W k P_{W_{k}}  so that  P W k ⊆ W k ∩ P V P_{W_{k}}\subseteq W_{k}\cap P_{V}  and so that they partition  P V ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V ) P_{V}\cap Z(f_{V}) . This is clearly possible since  P V ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V ) ⊂ V ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V ) = ⋃ k = 1 s W k P_{V}\cap Z(f_{V})\subset V\cap Z(f_{V})=\bigcup_{k=1}^{s}W_{k} . (There may be multiple ways to define these sets if some point lies in multiple of the  W k W_{k}  but we make these choices arbitrarily.) We place  ( W 1 , P W 1 ) , … , ( W s , P W s ) (W_{1},P_{W_{1}}),\ldots,(W_{s},P_{W_{s}})  into  𝒰 j − 1 \mathcal{U}_{j-1} . Note that since  { P C } C ∈ 𝒞 V \{P_{C}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}}  partitions  P V ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V ) P_{V}\setminus Z(f_{V})  and  { P W k } k ∈ [ s ] \{P_{W_{k}}\}_{k\in[s]}  partitions  P V ∩ Z ⁡ ( f V ) P_{V}\cap Z(f_{V}) , we see that  5  is still satisfied. Doing this procedure for each  ( V , 𝒫 V ) ∈ 𝒱 j (V,\mathcal{P}_{V})\in\mathcal{V}_{j}  completes step  j j . 
 
 
 We run this process for steps  j = d , d − 1 , … , 0 j=d,d-1,\ldots,0 . At the end we have produced the desired data. For  V ∈ 𝒱 i V\in\mathcal{V}_{i} , we chose  f part f_{\prt}  so that each connected component of  ℝ d ∖ Z ⁡ ( f part ) \mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Z(f_{\prt})  contains at most 
 

 
 
 ≲ d | P V | A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i δ j ​ ( V ) ≲ d | P V | A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i deg ⁡ Q j ( V ) \lesssim_{d}\frac{\left\lvert P_{V}\right\rvert}{A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\delta_{j}(V)}\lesssim_{d}\frac{\left\lvert P_{V}\right\rvert}{A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}} 
 
 
 points of  P V P_{V} . Since each connected components of  Z ⁡ ( Q ( V ) ) ​ ( ℝ ) ∖ Z ⁡ ( f V ) Z(Q^{(V)})(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z(f_{V})  is contained in one of these connected components, we see that  4  is satisfied. By  5 , we conclude that  3  is satisfied. Next note that for each  0 ≤ j ≤ d 0\leq j\leq d , the sets  { P V } V ∈ 𝒰 j \{P_{V}\}_{V\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}  are disjoint, implying  2  since  ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 | P V | ≤ ∑ j = 0 d ∑ ( V , P V ) ∈ 𝒰 j | P V | ≤ ( d + 1 ) ​ | P | \sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}|P_{V}|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{d}\sum_{(V,P_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}|P_{V}|\leq(d+1)|P| . Finally, since  deg f V ≲ d A \deg f_{V}\lesssim_{d}A  for each  V V , by Bézout’s theorem, we see that for each  0 ≤ j ≤ d 0\leq j\leq d , 
 

 
 
 ∑ ( V , P V ) ∈ 𝒰 j deg V ≲ d A d − j . \sum_{(V,P_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}\deg V\lesssim_{d}A^{d-j}. 
 
 
 By  Proposition   3.2 , we have 
 

 
 
 deg V ≳ d δ 1 ( V ) ⋯ δ d − j ( V ) ≳ d δ 1 ( V ) ⋯ δ d − i ( V ) A j − i ≳ d deg Q 1 ( V ) ⋯ deg Q d − i ( V ) A j − i \deg V\gtrsim_{d}\delta_{1}(V)\cdots\delta_{d-j}(V)\gtrsim_{d}\delta_{1}(V)\cdots\delta_{d-i}(V)A^{j-i}\gtrsim_{d}\deg Q_{1}^{(V)}\cdots\deg Q_{d-i}^{(V)}A^{j-i} 
 
 
 for every  ( V , P V ) ∈ 𝒰 j (V,P_{V})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}  with  i i  satisfying  δ d − i ​ ( V ) ≤ A < δ d − i + 1 ​ ( V ) \delta_{d-i}(V)\leq A<\delta_{d-i+1}(V) .
Combining the last two inequalities implies  1 , proving the desired result.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Turán results 

 
 In this section we prove  Corollary   1.5 . We deduce this from the following stronger result. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.1 . 
 
 Let  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  be a semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  and at least  ε ​ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | \varepsilon|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|  edges. Fix  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k 1\leq\ell\leq k . There exist sets  S 1 ⊆ P 1 , … , S ℓ ⊆ P ℓ S_{1}\subseteq P_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}\subseteq P_{\ell}  and  T ⊆ P ℓ + 1 × ⋯ × P k T\subseteq P_{\ell+1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}  such that  S 1 × ⋯ × S ℓ × T ⊆ E S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{\ell}\times T\subseteq E  and such that for  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell , 
 

 
 
 | S i | = Ω d , k ​ ( ( ε / D ) d ​ | P i | ) |S_{i}|=\Omega_{d,k}\left((\varepsilon/D)^{d}|P_{i}|\right) 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 | T | ≥ ε 3 ​ | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | . |T|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}|. 
 
 
 
 
 
 The  ℓ = k − 1 \ell=k-1  of this result immediately implies  Corollary   1.5 . In  Section   5  we will use the  ℓ = 1 \ell=1  case in the derivation of  Theorem   1.6 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First we handle the  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  case. Fix  d , k , ℓ d,k,\ell  and let  C = C ⁡ ( d , k ) C=C(d,k)  be a constant chosen sufficiently large in terms of the constant in  Theorem   1.3 . We will prove by induction on  | P 1 | + ⋯ + | P ℓ | |P_{1}|+\cdots+|P_{\ell}|  that we can find the desired sets  S 1 , … , S ℓ , T S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell},T  satisfying 
 

 
 
 | S i | ≥ 1 C ​ ( ε D ) d ​ | P i | |S_{i}|\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}|P_{i}| 
 
 
 for  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell  and 
 

 
 
 | T | ≥ ε 3 ​ | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | . |T|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}|. 
 
 
 
 
 The base case  | P 1 | + ⋯ + | P ℓ | = 0 \left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert+\cdots+\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert=0  is trivial.
For the inductive step, we apply  Theorem   1.3  to  H H  with parameter  ε / 3 \varepsilon/3  to produce partitions  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k} . For each  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell , let  B i B_{i}  be the union of parts  π ∈ Π i \pi\in\Pi_{i}  with  | π | ≤ 1 9 ​ k 2 ​ | P i | | Π i | \left\lvert\pi\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{9k^{2}}\frac{|P_{i}|}{|\Pi_{i}|} , i.e.,  B i B_{i}  is the union of the parts that are much smaller than average. 
 
 
 We split into two cases. If at most  ( ε / 3 ) | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | (\varepsilon/3)\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert  edges involve a vertex in  B 1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ B ℓ B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{\ell} , define  H ′ = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ′ ) H^{\prime}=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E^{\prime})  to be the subhypergraph of  H H  formed by removing those edges along with the edges contained in an inhomogeneous  k k -tuple of parts.
Then  | E ′ | ≥ ( ε / 3 ) | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | |E^{\prime}|\geq(\varepsilon/3)\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert . By the pigeonhole principle, there exists some  ℓ \ell -tuple  ( x 1 , … , x ℓ ) ∈ P 1 × ⋯ × P ℓ (x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\in P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{\ell}  such that 
 

 
 
 T = { ( x ℓ + 1 , … , x k ) ∈ P ℓ + 1 × ⋯ × P k : ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ E ′ } T=\{(x_{\ell+1},\ldots,x_{k})\in P_{\ell+1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}:(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in E^{\prime}\} 
 
 
 has size at least  ( ε / 3 ) ​ | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | (\varepsilon/3)|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}| . For  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell , define  S i ∈ Π i S_{i}\in\Pi_{i}  to be the part of the partition containing  x i x_{i} . We see that  S 1 × ⋯ × S ℓ × T ⊆ E ′ ⊆ E S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{\ell}\times T\subseteq E^{\prime}\subseteq E  and for  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell , 
 

 
 
 | S i | ≥ 1 9 ​ k 2 | P i | | Π i | ≳ d , k ( ε D ) d | P i | . |S_{i}|\geq\frac{1}{9k^{2}}\frac{|P_{i}|}{|\Pi_{i}|}\gtrsim_{d,k}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}|P_{i}|. 
 
 
 Choosing  C C  sufficiently large, this completes the induction in this case. 
 
 
 In the remaining case, there must be some  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell  such that the number of edges involving  B i B_{i}  is at least  ( ε / 3 k ) | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | (\varepsilon/3k)\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert . Define  H ′ = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B i ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ′ ) H^{\prime}=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{i}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E^{\prime})  to be the induced subhypergraph of  H H  on vertex set  P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B i ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{i}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k} . Note that 
 

 
 
 | B i | ≤ 1 9 ​ k 2 ​ | P i | | Π i | ​ | Π i | = | P i | 9 ​ k 2 . |B_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{9k^{2}}\frac{|P_{i}|}{|\Pi_{i}|}|\Pi_{i}|=\frac{|P_{i}|}{9k^{2}}. 
 
 
 Furthermore, the edge density of  H ′ H^{\prime}  is 
 

 
 
 η = | E ′ | | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ B i ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | ≥ ε 3 ​ k ​ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ B i ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | = ε 3 ​ k ⋅ | P i | | B i | ≥ 3 ​ k ​ ε > ε . \eta=\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{|P_{1}|\cdots|B_{i}|\cdots|P_{k}|}\geq\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{3k}|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|}{|P_{1}|\cdots|B_{i}|\cdots|P_{k}|}=\frac{\varepsilon}{3k}\cdot\frac{|P_{i}|}{|B_{i}|}\geq 3k\varepsilon>\varepsilon. 
 
 
 Since  | P 1 | + ⋯ + | B i | + ⋯ + | P k | < | P 1 | + ⋯ + | P k | |P_{1}|+\cdots+|B_{i}|+\cdots+|P_{k}|<|P_{1}|+\cdots+|P_{k}| , we can apply the inductive hypothesis to  H ′ H^{\prime} . This produces  S 1 ⊆ P 1 , … , S i ⊆ B i ⊂ P i , … , S ℓ ⊆ P ℓ S_{1}\subseteq P_{1},\ldots,S_{i}\subseteq B_{i}\subset P_{i},\ldots,S_{\ell}\subseteq P_{\ell}  and  T ⊆ P ℓ + 1 × ⋯ × P k T\subseteq P_{\ell+1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}  such that  S 1 × ⋯ × S ℓ × T ⊆ E ′ ⊆ E S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{\ell}\times T\subseteq E^{\prime}\subseteq E  and such that 
 

 
 
 | S j | ≥ 1 C ​ ( η D ) d ​ | P j | ≥ 1 C ​ ( ε D ) d ​ | P j | |S_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\eta}{D}\right)^{d}|P_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}|P_{j}| 
 
 
 for each  1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ 1\leq j\leq\ell  with  j ≠ i j\neq i . Additionally, 
 

 
 
 | S i | ≥ 1 C ​ ( η D ) d ​ | B i | ≥ 1 C ​ ( ε 3 ​ k ⋅ | P i | | B i | D ) d ​ | B i | = 1 C ​ ( ε D ) d ​ ( | P i | | B i | ) d − 1 ( 3 ​ k ) d ​ | P i | ≥ 1 C ​ ( ε D ) d ​ ( 9 ​ k 2 ) d − 1 ( 3 ​ k ) d ​ | P i | ≥ 1 C ​ ( ε D ) d ​ | P i | \begin{split}|S_{i}|\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\eta}{D}\right)^{d}|B_{i}|&\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{3k}\cdot\frac{|P_{i}|}{|B_{i}|}}{D}\right)^{d}|B_{i}|=\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}\frac{\left(\frac{|P_{i}|}{|B_{i}|}\right)^{d-1}}{(3k)^{d}}|P_{i}|\\
&\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}\frac{(9k^{2})^{d-1}}{(3k)^{d}}|P_{i}|\geq\frac{1}{C}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{d}|P_{i}|\end{split} 
 
 
 where the last inequality holds since  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 . Finally the inductive hypothesis also gives 
 

 
 
 | T | ≥ η 3 | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | ≥ ε 3 ​ | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | . |T|\geq\frac{\eta}{3}|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}|. 
 
 
 This completes the induction. 
 
 
 Now we prove the result for  d = 1 d=1 . Let  Π 1 , … , Π k \Pi_{1},\ldots,\Pi_{k}  be the equitable partitions formed by applying  Corollary   1.4  with error parameter  ε / 2 \varepsilon/2 . Note that since  d = 1 d=1 , we have  | Π i | = O k ​ ( D / ε ) |\Pi_{i}|=O_{k}(D/\varepsilon) . Let  H ′ = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ′ ) H^{\prime}=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E^{\prime})  be the subhypergraph of  H H  formed by removing the edges contained in an inhomogeneous  k k -tuple of parts. Clearly  | E ′ | ≥ ( ε / 2 ) | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | |E^{\prime}|\geq(\varepsilon/2)\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert . By the pigeonhole principle, there exists some  ℓ \ell -tuple  ( x 1 , … , x ℓ ) ∈ P 1 × ⋯ × P ℓ (x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\in P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{\ell}  such that 
 

 
 
 T = { ( x ℓ + 1 , … , x k ) ∈ P ℓ + 1 × ⋯ × P k : ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ E ′ } T=\{(x_{\ell+1},\ldots,x_{k})\in P_{\ell+1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}:(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in E^{\prime}\} 
 
 
 has size at least  ( ε / 2 ) ​ | P ℓ + 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | (\varepsilon/2)|P_{\ell+1}|\cdots|P_{k}| . For  1 ≤ i ≤ ℓ 1\leq i\leq\ell , define  S i ∈ Π i S_{i}\in\Pi_{i}  to be the part of the partition containing  x i x_{i} . We see that  S 1 × ⋯ × S ℓ × T ⊆ E ′ ⊆ E S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{\ell}\times T\subseteq E^{\prime}\subseteq E  and that  S 1 , … , S ℓ , T S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell},T  are of the appropriate sizes.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.  The Zarankiewicz problem 

 
 We prove the graph case ( k = 2 k=2 ) of the Zarankiewicz-type result first. The proof is essentially a specialization of the general proof, but the calculations are significantly simpler so we think it is valuable to read this case first. 
 
 
 The graph case is proved in two steps. First we deduce a weak Zarankiewicz result from our Turán result,  Corollary   1.5 . Then we upgrade the weak Zarankiewicz result to the strong one via multilevel polynomial partitioning,  Theorem   2.1 . These are the same two steps used by Fox, Pach, Sheffer, Suk, and Zahl in the proof of their semialgebraic Zarankiewicz result. They prove a similar weak Zarankiewicz result  [ 24 , Theorem 2.1]  using VC-dimension techniques and then upgrade it using constant-degree polynomial partitioning. Note in contrast, both halves of our proof use only polynomial partitioning and we avoid the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss by using polynomial partitioning using polynomials of fairly high degree. 
 
 
 One should compare the following result to the Kővári–Sós–Turán theorem which gives the bound of  O u ​ ( N 2 − 1 / u ) O_{u}(N^{2-1/u})  edges. Here we improve this to  O u , d ​ ( N 2 − 1 / d ) O_{u,d}(N^{2-1/d})  which is typically much smaller. 
 
 
 Corollary 5.1 . 
 
 Let  G = ( P ⊔ Q , E ) G=(P\sqcup Q,E)  be a semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D . If  G G  is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free, then the number of edges of  G G  is at most 
 

 
 
 | E | = O d ​ ( u 1 / d ​ D ​ | P | ​ | Q | 1 − 1 / d + u ​ | Q | ) . |E|=O_{d}\left(u^{1/d}D|P||Q|^{1-1/d}+u|Q|\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  | E | = ε ​ | P | ​ | Q | |E|=\varepsilon|P||Q| . By  Corollary   1.5 ,  G G  contains  T × S T\times S  with 
 

 
 
 | S | ≳ d ( ε / D ) d | Q | and | T | ≳ ε | P | . |S|\gtrsim_{d}(\varepsilon/D)^{d}|Q|\qquad\text{and}\qquad|T|\gtrsim\varepsilon|P|. 
 
 
 Since  G G  is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free we conclude that one of these sets is smaller than  u u . Thus either 
 

 
 
 ( ε / D ) d | Q | ≲ d u or ε | P | ≲ u . (\varepsilon/D)^{d}|Q|\lesssim_{d}u\qquad\text{or}\qquad\varepsilon|P|\lesssim u. 
 
 
 These inequalities rearrange to 
 

 
 
 | E | = ε | P | | Q | ≲ d D ( u | Q | ) 1 / d | P | | Q | or | E | ≲ u | Q | . ∎ |E|=\varepsilon|P||Q|\lesssim_{d}{D\left(\frac{u}{|Q|}\right)^{1/d}|P||Q|}\qquad\text{or}\qquad|E|\lesssim u|Q|.\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 5.2 . 
 
 Let  G = ( P ⊔ Q , E ) G=(P\sqcup Q,E)  be a semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D . If  G G  is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free, then the number of edges of  G G  is at most 
 

 
 
 | E | = O d ​ ( u 2 d + 1 ​ D 2 ​ d d + 1 ​ ( | P | ​ | Q | ) d d + 1 + u ​ | P | + u ​ | Q | ) . |E|=O_{d}\left(u^{\frac{2}{d+1}}D^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}(|P||Q|)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}+u|P|+u|Q|\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first deal with the case that  u d − 1 ​ | Q | > D d − 1 ​ | P | d u^{d-1}|Q|>D^{d-1}|P|^{d} .
In this case,  Corollary   5.1  gives the bound 
 

 
 
 | E | ≲ d u 1 / d D | P | | Q | 1 − 1 / d + u | Q | . |E|\lesssim_{d}u^{1/d}D|P||Q|^{1-1/d}+u|Q|. 
 
 
 We will upper bound the first term by  u 2 d + 1 ​ D ​ ( | P | ​ | Q | ) d d + 1 u^{\frac{2}{d+1}}D(|P||Q|)^{\frac{d}{d+1}} , which suffices as  1 ≤ 2 ​ d d + 1 1\leq\frac{2d}{d+1} .
To do so, simply note that 
 

 
 
 u 1 d ​ D ​ | P | ​ | Q | 1 − 1 d = u 1 d ​ D ​ ( | P | d | Q | ) 1 d ⁡ ( d + 1 ) ​ ( | P | ​ | Q | ) d d + 1 < u 2 d + 1 ​ D ​ ( | P | ​ | Q | ) d d + 1 u^{\frac{1}{d}}D|P||Q|^{1-\frac{1}{d}}=u^{\frac{1}{d}}D\left(\frac{\left\lvert P\right\rvert^{d}}{\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{1}{d(d+1)}}(|P||Q|)^{\frac{d}{d+1}}<u^{\frac{2}{d+1}}D(|P||Q|)^{\frac{d}{d+1}} 
 
 
 as  | P | d / | Q | < u d − 1 / D d − 1 ≤ u d − 1 \left\lvert P\right\rvert^{d}/\left\lvert Q\right\rvert<u^{d-1}/D^{d-1}\leq u^{d-1}  in this case. 
 
 
 Therefore we may now assume that  u d − 1 ​ | Q | ≤ D d − 1 ​ | P | d u^{d-1}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert\leq D^{d-1}\left\lvert P\right\rvert^{d} .
We apply multilevel polynomial partitioning,  Theorem   2.1 , to  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  with parameter 
 

 
 
 A = u − 1 / ( d + 1 ) D 1 / ( d + 1 ) | P | d / ( d 2 − 1 ) | Q | − 1 / ( d 2 − 1 ) . A=u^{-1/(d+1)}D^{1/(d+1)}|P|^{d/(d^{2}-1)}|Q|^{-1/(d^{2}-1)}. 
 
 
 Note that by assumption  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 . 
 
 
 For each  q ∈ Q q\in Q , consider the semialgebraic neighborhood set 
 

 
 
 N q = { x ∈ ℝ d : Φ ⁡ ( sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x , q ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x , q ) ) ) = 1 } . N_{q}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\Phi(\sgn(g_{1}(x,q)),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x,q)))=1\}. 
 
 
 For  p ∈ P p\in P  we have  ( p , q ) ∈ E (p,q)\in E  if and only if  p ∈ N q p\in N_{q} . 
 
 
 For each cell  C ∈ 𝒞 C\in\mathcal{C}  we have a set  P C P_{C}  produced by multilevel polynomial partitioning. Now for each cell, define a partition 
 

 
 
 Q = Q C , 𝗇𝗈𝗇𝖾 ⊔ Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ⊔ Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 Q=Q_{C,\mathsf{none}}\sqcup Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}\sqcup Q_{C,\mathsf{all}} 
 
 
 where  Q C , 𝗇𝗈𝗇𝖾 = { q ∈ Q : N q ∩ C = ∅ } Q_{C,\mathsf{none}}=\{q\in Q:N_{q}\cap C=\emptyset\}  and  Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 = { q ∈ Q : N q ∩ C = C } Q_{C,\mathsf{all}}=\{q\in Q:N_{q}\cap C=C\}  and the remaining vertices lie in  Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 Q_{C,\mathsf{some}} . Note in particular that  E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝗇𝗈𝗇𝖾 ] E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{none}}]  is empty and  E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{all}}]  is complete. Thus we can write 
 

 
 
 | E | = ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 | E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | + | E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] | . |E|=\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}]|+|E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{all}}]|. 
 
 
 
 
 We bound the first sum using  Corollary   5.1 . For  V ∈ 𝒱 V\in\mathcal{V} , define  i V ​ ( A ) i_{V}(A)  so that  V ∈ 𝒱 i V ​ ( A ) V\in\mathcal{V}_{i_{V}(A)} . Now 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 | E \displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|E 
 [ P C × Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | \displaystyle[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}]| 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V | E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | \displaystyle=\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}}|E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}]|

 [Hölder’s inequality]

 For  q ∈ Q q\in Q  and  V ∈ 𝒱 V\in\mathcal{V}  we count the number of cells  C ∈ 𝒞 V C\in\mathcal{C}_{V}  such that  q ∈ Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 q\in Q_{C,\mathsf{some}} . If  N q N_{q}  is neither entirely contained in nor entirely disjoint from  C C , we see that one of  sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x , q ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x , q ) ) \sgn(g_{1}(x,q)),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x,q))  is not constant on  C C . Thus by  Lemma   2.4  (applied with  k = 1 k=1  and  g ​ ( x ) = g r ​ ( x , q ) g(x)=g_{r}(x,q) ), we see that  q ∈ Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 q\in Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}  for at most  ≲ d D A i V ​ ( A ) − 1 ∏ j = 1 d − i V ​ ( A ) deg Q j ( V ) \lesssim_{d}DA^{i_{V}(A)-1}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{V}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}  choices of  C ∈ 𝒞 V C\in\mathcal{C}_{V} .  Combining this with the above inequalities gives 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 | E ⁡ [ P C × Q C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | \displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}|E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{some}}]| 
 ≲ ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 d ⁡ ( u 1 / d ​ D ​ | P V | ​ ( | Q | ​ D ​ A i V ​ ( A ) − 1 ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i V ​ ( A ) deg ⁡ Q j ( V ) A i V ​ ( A ) ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i V ​ ( A ) deg ⁡ Q j ( V ) ) 1 − 1 / d CLOSE \displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\left(u^{1/d}D|P_{V}|\left(\frac{|Q|DA^{i_{V}(A)-1}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{V}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}}{A^{i_{V}(A)}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{V}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}}\right)^{1-1/d}\right. 
 
 
 
 
 + u | Q | D A i V ​ ( A ) − 1 ∏ j = 1 d − i V ​ ( A ) deg Q j ( V ) ) \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+u|Q|DA^{i_{V}(A)-1}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{V}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 ≲ ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 d ⁡ ( u 1 / d ​ D 2 − 1 / d ​ | P V | ​ ( | Q | A ) 1 − 1 / d + u ​ | Q | ​ D ​ A i V ​ ( A ) − 1 ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i V ​ ( A ) deg ⁡ Q j ( V ) ) \displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\left(u^{1/d}D^{2-1/d}|P_{V}|\left(\frac{|Q|}{A}\right)^{1-1/d}+u|Q|DA^{i_{V}(A)-1}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{V}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V)}\right) 
 
 
 [by  2  and  1 ] 
 
 
 ≲ d u 1 / d D 2 − 1 / d | P | ( | Q | A ) 1 − 1 / d + u | Q | D A d − 1 . \displaystyle\lesssim_{d}u^{1/d}D^{2-1/d}|P|\left(\frac{|Q|}{A}\right)^{1-1/d}+u|Q|DA^{d-1}. 
 
 
 By our choice of  A A , both terms are  u 2 / ( d + 1 ) ​ D 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) ​ | P | d / ( d + 1 ) ​ | Q | d / ( d + 1 ) u^{2/(d+1)}D^{2d/(d+1)}|P|^{d/(d+1)}|Q|^{d/(d+1)} . 
 
 
 Now we bound the second sum. Note that since  G G  is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free, we have either  | P C | < u |P_{C}|<u  or  | Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 | < u |Q_{C,\mathsf{all}}|<u  for each  C ∈ 𝒞 C\in\mathcal{C} . We then have the easy bound

 < ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 : | P C | ≥ u u | P C | ≲ d u | P | . \displaystyle<\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}:|P_{C}|\geq u}u|P_{C}|\lesssim_{d}u|P|. 
 
 
 To finish, we define 
 

 
 
 P ′ = ⋃ C ∈ 𝒞 : | P C | < u P C P^{\prime}=\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}:|P_{C}|<u}P_{C} 
 
 
 so that 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 : | P C | < u | E [ P C × Q C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] | ≤ | E [ P ′ × Q ] | ≲ d u 1 / d D | P ′ | 1 − 1 / d | Q | + u | P ′ | \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}:|P_{C}|<u}|E[P_{C}\times Q_{C,\mathsf{all}}]|\leq|E[P^{\prime}\times Q]|\lesssim_{d}u^{1/d}D|P^{\prime}|^{1-1/d}|Q|+u|P^{\prime}| 
 
 
 by  Corollary   5.1 . 
 
 
 We have the bound  | P ′ | < u | 𝒞 | ≲ d u A d |P^{\prime}|<u|\mathcal{C}|\lesssim_{d}uA^{d}  by  Lemma   2.3  together with  1 . Using this bound and the trivial bound  | P ′ | ≤ | P | |P^{\prime}|\leq|P| , we see that the second sum is bounded by 
 

 
 
 O d ​ ( u 1 / d ​ D ​ ( u ​ A d ) 1 − 1 / d ​ | Q | + u ​ | P | ) . O_{d}\left(u^{1/d}D(uA^{d})^{1-1/d}|Q|+u|P|\right). 
 
 
 As the first term simplifies to  u ​ | Q | ​ D ​ A d − 1 u\left\lvert Q\right\rvert DA^{d-1} , which has already appeared, the proof is complete.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now we give the general proof. The proof is by induction on the uniformity  k k . Given the result for  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -uniform hypergraphs, we bootstrap it to a weak Zarankiewicz bound for  k k -uniform hypergraphs. This upgrading procedure uses our Turán result, specifically  Proposition   4.1  for  ℓ = 1 \ell=1 . Then we upgrade the weak bound to the full theorem via multilevel polynomial partitioning. The following is the full Zarankiewicz result. 
 
 
 Theorem 5.3 . 
 
 Let  H = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k , E ) H=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k},E)  be a semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D . If  H H  is  K u , … , u ( k ) K^{(k)}_{u,\ldots,u} -free, then the number of edges of  H H  is at most 
 

 
 
 | E | = O d , k ( | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | ∑ s = 1 k u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∑ I ∈ ( [ k ] s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) |E|=O_{d,k}\left(\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert\sum_{s=1}^{k}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k]}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}\right) 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 α s = ( s − 1 ) ​ ( s + 2 ) 2 and β s = ( s − 2 ) ​ ( s − 1 ) ​ ( s + 3 ) 6 . \alpha_{s}=\frac{(s-1)(s+2)}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{s}=\frac{(s-2)(s-1)(s+3)}{6}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We will prove the statement by induction on  k k . When  k = 1 k=1 , the result is trivial since a  K u ( 1 ) K_{u}^{(1)} -free 1-uniform hypergraph is exactly one satisfying  | E | < u |E|<u . Now assume that  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  and that the statement holds for  k − 1 k-1 . 
 
 
 We will first prove a weak Zarankiewicz result from the inductive hypothesis. Suppose that there are  ε | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | \varepsilon\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert  edges in  E E . By  Proposition   4.1  applied with  ℓ = 1 \ell=1 , there exists a set  S k ⊆ P k S_{k}\subseteq P_{k}  with size at least  ≳ d , k ( ε / D ) d | P k | \gtrsim_{d,k}(\varepsilon/D)^{d}\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert  and a set  T ⊆ P 1 × ⋯ × P k − 1 T\subseteq P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{k-1}  with size at least  ( ε / 3 ) | P 1 | ⋯ | P k − 1 | (\varepsilon/3)\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k-1}\right\rvert  such that  T × S k ⊆ E T\times S_{k}\subseteq E . 
 
 
 If  | S k | < u \left\lvert S_{k}\right\rvert<u , then  u > | S k | ≳ d , k ( ε / D ) d | P k | u>|S_{k}|\gtrsim_{d,k}(\varepsilon/D)^{d}|P_{k}| , implying  ε ≲ d , k u 1 / d D | P k | − 1 / d \varepsilon\lesssim_{d,k}u^{1/d}D\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert^{-1/d} .
Now if  | S k | ≥ u \left\lvert S_{k}\right\rvert\geq u , choose  u u  distinct elements  q 1 , … , q u ∈ S k q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}\in S_{k}  arbitrarily and define the following  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -uniform semialgebraic hypergraph  ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k − 1 , T ′ ) (P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k-1},T^{\prime}) . For each  q ∈ P k q\in P_{k}  define the semialgebraic neighborhood  N q ⊆ ( ℝ d ) k − 1 N_{q}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k-1}  to be 
 

 
 
 N q = { x ∈ ( ℝ d ) k − 1 : Φ ⁡ ( sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x , q ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x , q ) ) ) = 1 } . N_{q}=\{x\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k-1}:\Phi(\sgn(g_{1}(x,q)),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x,q)))=1\}. 
 
 
 Then let  T ′ T^{\prime}  be the set of  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -tuples in  P 1 × ⋯ × P k − 1 P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{k-1}  that lie in  N q 1 ∩ ⋯ ∩ N q u N_{q_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap N_{q_{u}} .
It is clear that  T ′ T^{\prime}  is the edge set of a  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -uniform semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree at most  u ​ D uD .
We also know that  T ⊆ T ′ T\subseteq T^{\prime} .
Finally, since  T ′ T^{\prime}  is the common neighborhood of  q 1 , … , q u q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}  we know that  T ′ T^{\prime}  is  K u , … , u ( k − 1 ) K^{(k-1)}_{u,\ldots,u} -free.
Therefore by the inductive hypothesis, 
 

 
 
 | T | ≤ | T ′ | ≲ d , k | P 1 | ⋯ | P k − 1 | ∑ s = 1 k − 1 u s + ( β s + ( k − 1 − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ( u D ) α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∑ I ∈ ( [ k − 1 ] s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 . |T|\leq\left\lvert T^{\prime}\right\rvert\lesssim_{d,k}\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k-1}\right\rvert\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-1-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}(uD)^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k-1]}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}. 
 
 
 As a consequence, 
 

 
 
 ε ≲ ∑ s = 1 k − 1 d , k ⁡ u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ ∑ I ∈ ( [ k − 1 ] s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 . \varepsilon\lesssim_{d,k}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k-1]}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}. 
 
 
 Combining the cases where  | S k | ≥ u \left\lvert S_{k}\right\rvert\geq u  and where  | S k | < u \left\lvert S_{k}\right\rvert<u , we deduce that 
 

 
 (6) 
 
 | E | ≲ d , k u 1 / d D | P 1 | ⋯ | P k − 1 | | P k | 1 − 1 / d + | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | ∑ s = 1 k − 1 u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∑ I ∈ ( [ k − 1 ] s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 . \begin{split}|E|\lesssim_{d,k}u^{1/d}D&\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k-1}\right\rvert\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert^{1-1/d}\\
&+\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k-1]}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}.\end{split} 
 
 
 Note that this bound is too weak to deduce the desired result because the first term is potentially too large. However, the second term is smaller than the desired bound. 
 
 
 Now we upgrade this weak Zarankiewicz result to prove the desired bound for uniformity  k k . Apply multilevel polynomial partitioning,  Theorem   2.1 , to  P 1 , … , P k − 1 P_{1},\ldots,P_{k-1}  with some parameter  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1  that will be specified later. This produces data  𝒱 ( 1 ) , … , 𝒱 ( k − 1 ) \mathcal{V}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathcal{V}^{(k-1)} . 
 
 
 For  V 1 ∈ 𝒱 ( 1 ) , … , V k − 1 ∈ 𝒱 ( k − 1 ) V_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{(1)},\ldots,V_{k-1}\in\mathcal{V}^{(k-1)}  and each tuple of cells  C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}} , define the partition 
 

 
 
 P k = P C , 𝗇𝗈𝗇𝖾 ⊔ P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ⊔ P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 P_{k}=P_{C,\mathsf{none}}\sqcup P_{C,\mathsf{some}}\sqcup P_{C,\mathsf{all}} 
 
 
 as before:  P C , 𝗇𝗈𝗇𝖾 P_{C,\mathsf{none}}  consists of points  q ∈ P k q\in P_{k}  with  N q ∩ C = ∅ N_{q}\cap C=\emptyset  and  P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 P_{C,\mathsf{all}}  consists of points  q ∈ P k q\in P_{k}  with  N q ∩ C = C N_{q}\cap C=C  and  P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 P_{C,\mathsf{some}}  consists of the remaining points in  P k P_{k} .
We get 
 

 
 
 | E | = ∑ C | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | + | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] | . |E|=\sum_{C}|E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times P_{C,\mathsf{some}}]|+|E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times P_{C,\mathsf{all}}]|. 
 
 
 
 
 To deal with the first term we apply the weak Zarankiewicz result. This gives the upper bound 
 

 
 
 | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | ≲ d , k E 0 ( C ) + ∑ ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] E I ( C ) |E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times P_{C,\mathsf{some}}]|\lesssim_{d,k}E_{0}(C)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]}E_{I}(C) 
 
 
 where we define 
 

 
 
 E 0 ( C ) = u 1 / d D | ( P 1 ) C 1 | ⋯ | ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 | | P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 | 1 − 1 / d E_{0}(C)=u^{1/d}D\left\lvert(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert P_{C,\mathsf{some}}\right\rvert^{1-1/d} 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 E I ( C ) = | ( P 1 ) C 1 | ⋯ | ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 | | P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 | u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ( ∏ i ∈ I | ( P i ) C i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 E_{I}(C)=\left\lvert(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert P_{C,\mathsf{some}}\right\rvert u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert(P_{i})_{C_{i}}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}} 
 
 
 for each tuple of cells  C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}  and each  I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] I\subseteq[k-1]  with  | I | = s ≠ 0 |I|=s\neq 0 . 
 
 
 First fix a choice  V = ( V 1 , … , V k − 1 ) ∈ 𝒱 i 1 ( 1 ) × ⋯ × 𝒱 i k − 1 ( k − 1 ) V=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{k-1})\in\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{V}^{(k-1)}_{i_{k-1}} . We will bound the sum over  C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}} . By  4 , we know that 
 

 
 
 | ( P ℓ ) C ℓ | ≲ d | ( P ℓ ) V ℓ | A i ℓ ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg ⁡ Q j ( V ℓ ) \left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{C_{\ell}}\right\rvert\lesssim_{d}\frac{\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{V_{\ell}}\right\rvert}{A^{i_{\ell}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}} 
 
 
 for each  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k − 1 1\leq\ell\leq k-1 . For each  q ∈ P k q\in P_{k} , we count the number of cells  C C  so that  q ∈ P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 q\in P_{C,\mathsf{some}} . This only occurs if there exists some  1 ≤ r ≤ t 1\leq r\leq t  so that  sgn ⁡ ( g r ​ ( x 11 , … , x k − 1 , d , q 1 , … , q d ) ) \sgn(g_{r}(x_{11},\ldots,x_{k-1,d},q_{1},\ldots,q_{d}))  is not constant on  C ⊆ ℝ ( k − 1 ) ​ d C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{(k-1)d} . Applying  Lemma   2.4  to bound the number of such cells and summing over  r r  and  q ∈ P k q\in P_{k} , we conclude that 
 

 
 (7) 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 | P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 | ≲ d , k | P k | D A i 1 + ⋯ + i k − 1 − 1 ∏ ℓ = 1 k − 1 ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ ​ ( A ) deg Q j ( V ℓ ) \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}}|P_{C,\mathsf{some}}|\lesssim_{d,k}\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert DA^{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k-1}-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}(A)}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})} 
 
 
 for each  V ∈ 𝒱 ( 1 ) i 1 × ⋯ × 𝒱 ( k − 1 ) i k − 1 V\in\mathcal{V}^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{V}^{(k-1)}_{i_{k-1}} . 
 
 
 Thus we get the bound 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 E 0 ( C ) \displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}}E_{0}(C) 
 ≲ d , k u 1 / d D ( ∏ ℓ = 1 k − 1 | ( P ℓ ) V ℓ | A i ℓ ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg ⁡ Q j ( V ℓ ) ) 1 − 1 / d \displaystyle\lesssim_{d,k}u^{1/d}D\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\frac{\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{V_{\ell}}\right\rvert}{A^{i_{\ell}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}}\right)^{1-1/d} 
 
 
 
 
 ⋅ ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 ( ∏ ℓ = 1 k − 1 | ( P ℓ ) C ℓ | ) 1 / d | P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 | 1 − 1 / d \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}}\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{C_{\ell}}\right\rvert\right)^{1/d}\left\lvert P_{C,\mathsf{some}}\right\rvert^{1-1/d}

 [Hölder’s inequality] 
 
 
 ⋅ ( ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 | P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 | ) 1 − 1 / d \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}}\left\lvert P_{C,\mathsf{some}}\right\rvert\right)^{1-1/d}

 [by  7 ]

 Let  F ⁡ ( A ) F(A)  denote the expression on the right-hand side above.

&\cdot\prod_{\ell\in[k-1]\setminus I}\frac{\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{V_{\ell}}\right\rvert}{A^{i_{\ell}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}}.\end{split}

 For each  ℓ ∈ I \ell\in I , we know that
