 = ∑ i ℓ = 0 d ∑ V ℓ ∈ 𝒱 i ℓ ( ℓ ) | ( P ℓ ) V ℓ | 1 − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ ( A i ℓ ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg ⁡ Q j ( V ℓ ) ) 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 \displaystyle=\sum_{i_{\ell}=0}^{d}\sum_{V_{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{(\ell)}_{i_{\ell}}}\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{V_{\ell}}\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}\left(A^{i_{\ell}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}\right)^{\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}} 
 
 
 [Hölder’s inequality] 
 
 
 ≤ ∑ i ℓ = 0 d ( ∑ V ℓ ∈ 𝒱 i ℓ ( ℓ ) | ( P ℓ ) V ℓ | ) 1 − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ ( ∑ V ℓ ∈ 𝒱 i ℓ ( ℓ ) A i ℓ ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i ℓ deg ⁡ Q j ( V ℓ ) ) 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 \displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{\ell}=0}^{d}\left(\sum_{V_{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{(\ell)}_{i_{\ell}}}\left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{V_{\ell}}\right\rvert\right)^{1-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}\left(\sum_{V_{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{(\ell)}_{i_{\ell}}}A^{i_{\ell}}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i_{\ell}}\deg Q_{j}^{(V_{\ell})}\right)^{\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}} 
 
 
 [by  2  and  1 ] 
 
 
 ≲ ∑ i ℓ = 0 d d ⁡ | P ℓ | 1 − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ ( A d ) 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 \displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\sum_{i_{\ell}=0}^{d}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}\left(A^{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}} 
 
 
 
 
 ≲ d | P ℓ | ( A d | P ℓ | ) 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 . \displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert\left(\frac{A^{d}}{\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}. 
 
 
 
 
 Applying the above for  ℓ ∈ I \ell\in I  and applying  2  for  ℓ ∉ I \ell\not\in I , we conclude that 
 

 
 
 ∑ V ∈ 𝒱 ( 1 ) × ⋯ × 𝒱 ( k − 1 ) \displaystyle\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{V}^{(k-1)}} 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 V 1 × ⋯ × 𝒞 V k − 1 E I ( C ) \displaystyle\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{V_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{V_{k-1}}}E_{I}(C) 
 
 
 
 
 ≲ d , k | P k | u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D 1 + ( α s + 1 − 2 ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 A − 1 ∏ ℓ ∈ I | P ℓ | ( A d | P ℓ | ) 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∏ ℓ ∈ [ k − 1 ] ∖ I | P ℓ | \displaystyle\lesssim_{d,k}|P_{k}|u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{1+(\alpha_{s+1}-2)d}{(s-1)d+1}}A^{-1}\prod_{\ell\in I}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert\left(\frac{A^{d}}{\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}\prod_{\ell\in[k-1]\setminus I}|P_{\ell}| 
 
 
 
 
 = | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D 1 + ( α s + 1 − 2 ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 A d − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∏ ℓ ∈ I | P ℓ | − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 \displaystyle=\left\lvert P_{1}\right\rvert\cdots\left\lvert P_{k}\right\rvert u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{1+(\alpha_{s+1}-2)d}{(s-1)d+1}}A^{\frac{d-1}{(s-1)d+1}}\prod_{\ell\in I}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}} 
 
 
 for each  I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] I\subseteq[k-1]  with  | I | = s ≠ 0 |I|=s\neq 0 . Let  G I ​ ( A ) G_{I}(A)  denote the right-hand side above. 
 
 
 What we have shown is that for each  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 , 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 ( 1 ) × ⋯ × 𝒞 ( k − 1 ) | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝗌𝗈𝗆𝖾 ] | ≲ d , k F ( A ) + ∑ ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] G I ( A ) . \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}^{(k-1)}}|E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times P_{C,\mathsf{some}}]|\lesssim_{d,k}F(A)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]}G_{I}(A). 
 
 
 It remains to bound  ∑ C | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] | \sum_{C}|E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times P_{C,\mathsf{all}}]| .
Note that for each  C C , either there exists  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k − 1 1\leq\ell\leq k-1  with  | ( P ℓ ) C ℓ | < u \left\lvert(P_{\ell})_{C_{\ell}}\right\rvert<u  or  | P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 | < u \left\lvert P_{C,\mathsf{all}}\right\rvert<u . Define 
 

 
 
 P ℓ ′ = ⋃ C ℓ ∈ 𝒞 ( ℓ ) : | ( P ℓ ) C ℓ | < u ( P ℓ ) C ℓ P^{\prime}_{\ell}=\bigcup_{C_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}^{(\ell)}:|(P_{\ell})_{C_{\ell}}|<u}(P_{\ell})_{C_{\ell}} 
 
 
 for each  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k − 1 1\leq\ell\leq k-1 . It is then clear that 
 

 
 
 ∑ C ∈ 𝒞 ( 1 ) × ⋯ × 𝒞 ( k − 1 ) | E [ ( P 1 ) C 1 × ⋯ × ( P k − 1 ) C k − 1 × P C , 𝖺𝗅𝗅 ] | < u | P 1 | ⋯ | P k − 1 | + ∑ ℓ = 1 k − 1 | E [ P 1 × ⋯ × P ′ ℓ × ⋯ × P k − 1 × P k ] | . \begin{split}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}^{(k-1)}}|E[(P_{1})_{C_{1}}\times\cdots\times(P_{k-1})_{C_{k-1}}\times&P_{C,\mathsf{all}}]|<u|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k-1}|\\
&+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}|E[P_{1}\times\cdots\times P^{\prime}_{\ell}\times\cdots\times P_{k-1}\times P_{k}]|.\end{split} 
 
 
 Note that the first term is one that appears on the right-hand side of the desired bound. (It is the  I = { k } I=\{k\}  term.) We now bound the second term. 
 
 
 As  | 𝒞 ( ℓ ) | ≲ d A d |\mathcal{C}^{(\ell)}|\lesssim_{d}A^{d}  by  Lemmas   2.3  and  1 , we see that  | P ℓ ′ | ≲ d u A d \left\lvert P^{\prime}_{\ell}\right\rvert\lesssim_{d}uA^{d}  for each  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k − 1 1\leq\ell\leq k-1 . We also have the trivial bound  | P ℓ ′ | ≤ | P ℓ | |P^{\prime}_{\ell}|\leq|P_{\ell}| . We now apply the weak Zarankiewicz bound,  6 , with  P ℓ ′ P_{\ell}^{\prime}  as the last part. This gives 
 

 
 
 | E [ P 1 × ⋯ × P ℓ ′ × ⋯ \displaystyle|E[P_{1}\times\cdots\times P^{\prime}_{\ell}\times\cdots 
 × P k − 1 × P k ] | ≲ d , k u 1 / d D ( u A d ) 1 − 1 / d ∏ i ∈ [ k ] ∖ { ℓ } | P i | \displaystyle\times P_{k-1}\times P_{k}]|\lesssim_{d,k}u^{1/d}D(uA^{d})^{1-1/d}\prod_{i\in[k]\setminus\{\ell\}}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert 
 
 
 
 
 + ∏ i ∈ [ k ] | P i | ⋅ ∑ s = 1 k − 1 u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∑ I ∈ ( [ k ] ∖ { ℓ } s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 . \displaystyle+\prod_{i\in[k]}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\cdot\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k]\setminus\{\ell\}}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}. 
 
 
 Note that the second term consists only of terms that appear on the right-hand side of the desired bound. For the first term, note that it simplifies to  u ​ D ​ A d − 1 ​ ∏ i ≠ ℓ | P i | uDA^{d-1}\prod_{i\neq\ell}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert  which is equal to  G { ℓ } ​ ( A ) G_{\{\ell\}}(A) . Putting everything together, we have shown that if there exists  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1  so that 
 

 
 
 F ( A ) + ∑ ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] G I ( A ) ≲ d , k | P 1 | ⋯ | P k | ∑ s = 1 k u s + ( β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s ) ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 D α s ​ d ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ∑ I ∈ ( [ k ] s ) ( ∏ i ∈ I | P i | ) − 1 ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 , F(A)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]}G_{I}(A)\lesssim_{d,k}|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|\sum_{s=1}^{k}u^{\frac{s+(\beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s})d}{(s-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s}d}{(s-1)d+1}}\sum_{I\in\binom{[k]}{s}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{(s-1)d+1}}, 
 
 
 then we can close the induction. 
 
 
 Observe that as a function of  A A , the map  F : ℝ > 0 → ℝ > 0 F\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}  is decreasing and surjective while  G I : ℝ > 0 → ℝ > 0 G_{I}\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}  is increasing and surjective for all  ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] \emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1] . Thus for every  ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] \emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]  there exists a unique  A I > 0 A_{I}>0  so that  F ⁡ ( A I ) = G I ​ ( A I ) F(A_{I})=G_{I}(A_{I}) . Set  | I | = s ∈ [ k − 1 ] \left\lvert I\right\rvert=s\in[k-1] . From the fact that  β s + ( k − s ) ​ α s = β s + 1 + ( k − ( s + 1 ) ) ​ α s ≤ β s + 1 + ( k − ( s + 1 ) ) ​ α s + 1 \beta_{s}+(k-s)\alpha_{s}=\beta_{s+1}+(k-(s+1))\alpha_{s}\leq\beta_{s+1}+(k-(s+1))\alpha_{s+1} , we can compute 
 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( A I ) \displaystyle F(A_{I}) 
 = F ​ ( A I ) d s ​ d + 1 ​ G I ​ ( A I ) ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 s ​ d + 1 \displaystyle=F(A_{I})^{\frac{d}{sd+1}}G_{I}(A_{I})^{\frac{(s-1)d+1}{sd+1}} 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | u ( s + 1 ) + ( β s + 1 + ( k − ( s + 1 ) ) ​ α s + 1 ) ​ d s ​ d + 1 ​ D α s + 1 ​ d s ​ d + 1 ​ ( ∏ ℓ ∈ I ∪ { k } | P ℓ | ) − 1 s ​ d + 1 , \displaystyle\leq|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|u^{\frac{(s+1)+(\beta_{s+1}+(k-(s+1))\alpha_{s+1})d}{sd+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s+1}d}{sd+1}}\left(\prod_{\ell\in I\cup\{k\}}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{sd+1}}, 
 
 
 which is the term corresponding to  I ∪ { k } I\cup\{k\}  on the right-hand side of the desired bound.
Set  A = min ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] ⁡ A I A=\min_{\emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]}A_{I} . If  A ≥ 1 A\geq 1 , then we have  G I ​ ( A I ) = F ⁡ ( A I ) ≤ F ⁡ ( A ) G_{I}(A_{I})=F(A_{I})\leq F(A)  for all  ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] \emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]  and we also have that  F ⁡ ( A ) F(A)  is equal to the  I ∪ { k } I\cup\{k\}  term on the right-hand side of the desired bound where  I I  minimizes  A I A_{I} . 
 
 
 Therefore, it remains to deal with the case where  A < 1 A<1 . In this case, we can find  ∅ ≠ I ⊆ [ k − 1 ] \emptyset\neq I\subseteq[k-1]  with  A I < 1 A_{I}<1 . Set  | I | = s ≠ 0 |I|=s\neq 0 . We know that  F ⁡ ( 1 ) < F ⁡ ( A I ) = G I ​ ( A I ) < G I ​ ( 1 ) F(1)<F(A_{I})=G_{I}(A_{I})<G_{I}(1)  and so 
 

 
 
 u 1 d ​ D | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k − 1 | | P k | 1 − 1 d \displaystyle u^{\frac{1}{d}}D|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k-1}||P_{k}|^{1-\frac{1}{d}} 
 ≤ F ⁡ ( 1 ) < F ​ ( 1 ) d s ​ d + 1 ​ G I ​ ( 1 ) ( s − 1 ) ​ d + 1 s ​ d + 1 \displaystyle\leq F(1)<F(1)^{\frac{d}{sd+1}}G_{I}(1)^{\frac{(s-1)d+1}{sd+1}} 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ | P 1 ​ | ⋯ | ​ P k | u ( s + 1 ) + ( β s + 1 + ( k − ( s + 1 ) ) ​ α s + 1 ) ​ d s ​ d + 1 ​ D α s + 1 ​ d s ​ d + 1 ​ ( ∏ ℓ ∈ I ∪ { k } | P ℓ | ) − 1 s ​ d + 1 . \displaystyle\leq|P_{1}|\cdots|P_{k}|u^{\frac{(s+1)+(\beta_{s+1}+(k-(s+1))\alpha_{s+1})d}{sd+1}}D^{\frac{\alpha_{s+1}d}{sd+1}}\left(\prod_{\ell\in I\cup\{k\}}\left\lvert P_{\ell}\right\rvert\right)^{-\frac{1}{sd+1}}. 
 
 
 In this case, we apply the weak Zarankiewicz bound,  6 , to the whole hypergraph  H H . The above inequality show that the first term in that bound is less than one of the terms in the right-hand side of the desired bound while we already noted that all the other terms in that bound are sufficiently small to deduced the desired inequality. This completes the induction.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Finally, the symmetric version stated in the introduction follows immediately from this result. 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   1.6 . 
 
 The desired bound for general  k k  follows from  Theorem   5.3  by setting  | P 1 | = ⋯ = | P k | = N |P_{1}|=\cdots=|P_{k}|=N . Now specializing to  k = 2 k=2  gives 
 

 
 
 | E | ≲ d N 2 ( ( u N ) + D 2 ​ d d + 1 ( u N ) 2 d + 1 ) . \left\lvert E\right\rvert\lesssim_{d}N^{2}\left(\left(\frac{u}{N}\right)+D^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}\left(\frac{u}{N}\right)^{\frac{2}{d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 Note that it suffices to prove the claimed bound for  u ≤ N u\leq N  since otherwise it is weaker than the trivial bound  | E | ≤ N 2 |E|\leq N^{2} . Then since  D ≥ 1 D\geq 1  and  u / N ≤ 1 u/N\leq 1 , the second term above dominates, giving the claimed bound.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.  Additional applications 

 
 6.1.  The Erdős unit distance problem 

 
 The Erdős unit distance problem asks for the maximum number of pairs of points at distance 1 among a set of  N N  points in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . For  d = 2 , 3 d=2,3 , it is conjectured that an appropriately scaled section of the integer lattice asymptotically maximizes the number of unit distances. For  d ≥ 4 d\geq 4  a set of  N N  points can span  Θ ⁡ ( N 2 ) \Theta(N^{2})  unit distances. This can be achieved by placing the points on two circles lying in orthogonal planes. One can make this problem non-trivial by ruling out this configuration. This was studied by Oberlin and Oberlin who proved a bound of  O d ​ ( N 2 − 1 / d ) O_{d}(N^{2-1/d})  if no  d d  of the points are contained in a  ( d − 2 ) (d-2) -dimensional affine subspace  [ 44 ] . 
 
 
 Under the weaker restriction that the unit distance graph is  K u , u K_{u,u} -free, a better bound follows from the semialgebraic Zarankiewicz machinery. From the work of Fox–Pach–Sheffer–Suk–Zahl one deduces the bound of  O d , u ​ ( N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d,u}(N^{2d/(d+1)+o(1)})  unit distances  [ 24 ] . Do  [ 16 ]  improved this to  O d ​ ( u ​ N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d}(uN^{2d/(d+1)+o(1)}) . Frankl and Kupavskii  [ 27 ] , using cutting techniques that are tailored to the unit distance graph, improved the bound to  O d ​ ( u 2 / ( d + 1 ) ​ N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) O_{d}(u^{2/(d+1)}N^{2d/(d+1)+o(1)}) . As a corollary of our semialgebraic Zarankiewicz result,  Theorem   1.6 , we remove the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) -loss in the exponent. Furthermore we give a short geometric argument that any  K u , u K_{u,u}  in the unit distance graph must come from a pair of orthogonal spheres. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.1 . 
 
 Let  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  be a set of  N N  points so that every sphere of dimension  ⌊ d / 2 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1  contains fewer than  u u  points of  P P . Then the number of unit distances spanned by  P P  is at most 
 

 
 
 O d ​ ( u 2 d + 1 ​ N 2 ​ d d + 1 ) . O_{d}\left(u^{\frac{2}{d+1}}N^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Define  G G  to be the bipartite graph where each vertex set is a copy of  P P  and there is an edge between  p p  and  q q  if they are at distance 1. Clearly  G G  is a semialgebraic graph of total degree 2. Suppose for contradiction that  p 1 , … , p u , q 1 , … , q u p_{1},\ldots,p_{u},q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}  form a copy of  K u , u K_{u,u}  in  G G . 
 
 
 Let  V V  be the affine subspace of  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  spanned by  p 1 , … , p u p_{1},\ldots,p_{u} . Suppose that  dim V = r \dim V=r  and without loss of generality, suppose that  V V  is the affine span of  p 1 , … , p r + 1 p_{1},\ldots,p_{r+1} .
For each  i ∈ [ r ] i\in[r] , define the hyperplane  H i ⊆ V H_{i}\subseteq V  to be the perpendicular bisector of the segment  p i ​ p r + 1 ¯ \overline{p_{i}p_{r+1}} .
As  p 1 , … , p r + 1 p_{1},\ldots,p_{r+1}  affinely span the  r r -dimensional affine subspace  V V , we see that the normal vectors to  H 1 , … , H r H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}  are linearly independent, showing that they intersect at a unique point  v v . It is then clear that  v v  is equidistant from  p 1 , … , p r + 1 p_{1},\ldots,p_{r+1} . 
 
 
 Without loss of generality, assume that  v v  is the origin. Let  W W  be the orthogonal complement of  V V  (i.e., the subspace of dimension  d − r d-r  which is orthogonal to  V V  and passes through the origin). We claim that if  q q  is equidistant from  p 1 , … , p r + 1 p_{1},\ldots,p_{r+1} , then it must lie in  W W . Write  q = q V + q W q=q_{V}+q_{W}  where  q V ∈ V q_{V}\in V  and  q W ∈ W q_{W}\in W . Then 
 

 
 
 ‖ p i − q ‖ 2 = ‖ p i − q V ‖ 2 + ‖ q W ‖ 2 . \|p_{i}-q\|^{2}=\|p_{i}-q_{V}\|^{2}+\|q_{W}\|^{2}. 
 
 
 In particular,  q V ∈ V q_{V}\in V  is equidistant from  p 1 , … , p r + 1 p_{1},\ldots,p_{r+1} , implying that  q V ∈ H 1 ∩ ⋯ ∩ H r = { 0 } q_{V}\in H_{1}\cap\cdots\cap H_{r}=\{0\} . Thus  q = q W ∈ W q=q_{W}\in W , as desired. 
 
 
 Let  α = ‖ p 1 ‖ = ⋯ = ‖ p r + 1 ‖ \alpha=\|p_{1}\|=\cdots=\|p_{r+1}\| . For  j ∈ [ u ] j\in[u] , we know that  q j ∈ W q_{j}\in W  so in particular, 
 

 
 
 1 = ‖ p 1 − q j ‖ 2 = ‖ p 1 ‖ 2 + ‖ q j ‖ 2 = α 2 + ‖ q j ‖ 2 . 1=\|p_{1}-q_{j}\|^{2}=\|p_{1}\|^{2}+\|q_{j}\|^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\|q_{j}\|^{2}. 
 
 
 Thus  q 1 , … , q u q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}  all lie on the  ( d − r − 1 ) (d-r-1) -dimensional sphere of radius  β \beta  in  W W  where  α 2 + β 2 = 1 \alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=1 . 
 
 
 For  i ∈ [ u ] i\in[u] , we know that  p i ∈ V p_{i}\in V  by assumption. Then 
 

 
 
 1 = ‖ p i − q 1 ‖ 2 = ‖ p i ‖ 2 + ‖ q 1 ‖ 2 = ‖ p i ‖ 2 + β 2 . 1=\|p_{i}-q_{1}\|^{2}=\|p_{i}\|^{2}+\|q_{1}\|^{2}=\|p_{i}\|^{2}+\beta^{2}. 
 
 
 Thus  p 1 , … , p u p_{1},\ldots,p_{u}  all lie on the  ( r − 1 ) (r-1) -dimensional sphere of radius  α \alpha  in  V V . Clearly one of these two spheres has dimension at most  ⌊ d / 2 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1 , contradicting our assumption on  P P .
Now the desired bound follows from  Theorem   1.6 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 When  d = 2 , 3 d=2,3 ,  Proposition   6.1  applies to any set of  N N  points with  u = 3 u=3 . For  d = 2 d=2  this recovers  O ⁡ ( N 4 / 3 ) O(N^{4/3}) , the best-known upper bound  [ 50 ] . For  d = 3 d=3  this gives the bound of  O ⁡ ( N 3 / 2 ) O(N^{3/2})  which was the best-known bound  [ 13 ,  32 ,  58 ]  until recent work of Zahl  [ 60 ]  that used curve cutting to improve the bound to  N 3 / 2 − 1 / 394 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) N^{3/2-1/394+o(1)} . 
 
 
 Example 6.2 . 
 
 Let  P P  be a  N 1 / d × ⋯ × N 1 / d N^{1/d}\times\cdots\times N^{1/d}  section of the integer lattice, appropriately scaled. By the pigeonhole principle, there exists a scaling so that  P P  spans  ≳ d N 2 − 2 / d \gtrsim_{d}N^{2-2/d}  unit distances. By Schwartz–Zippel for varieties (see, e.g,  [ 10 , Lemma 14] ), every sphere of dimension  ⌊ d / 2 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1  contains at most  ≲ d N ( ⌊ d / 2 ⌋ − 1 ) / d \lesssim_{d}N^{(\left\lfloor d/2\right\rfloor-1)/d}  points of  P P . Thus  Proposition   6.1  gives an upper bound of 
 

 
 
 ≲ d ( N ( ⌊ d 2 ⌋ − 1 ) / d ) 2 d + 1 N 2 ​ d d + 1 = { N 2 − d + 2 d ⁡ ( d + 1 ) if  ​ d ​  is even , N 2 − d + 3 d ⁡ ( d + 1 ) if  ​ d ​  is odd . \lesssim_{d}\left(N^{\left(\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor-1\right)/d}\right)^{\frac{2}{d+1}}N^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}=\begin{cases}N^{2-\frac{d+2}{d(d+1)}}\quad&\text{if }d\text{ is even},\\
N^{2-\frac{d+3}{d(d+1)}}&\text{if }d\text{ is odd}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 This is fairly far from the lower bound even for large  d d , i.e., the upper bound approaches  N 2 − 1 / d N^{2-1/d}  for large  d d  while the lower bound is  N 2 − 2 / d N^{2-2/d} . 
 
 
 
 We can apply the same argument to bound the number of equilateral triangles contained in a set of  N N  points as long as the points do not cluster on a sphere of dimension  ⌊ d / 3 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/3\right\rfloor-1 . Note that this proof bounds not just the number of unit equilateral triangles, but the total number of equilateral triangles of all side lengths. This argument also easily adapts to bound the number of similar copies of any fixed triangle or more generally any fixed simplex of any dimension. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.3 . 
 
 Let  P ⊂ ℝ d P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}  be a set of  N N  points so that every sphere of dimension  ⌊ d / 3 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/3\right\rfloor-1  contains fewer than  u u  points of  P P . Then the number of equilateral triangles spanned by  P P  is at most 
 

 
 
 O d ​ ( u 2 ​ N 3 ​ d d + 1 + u 2 ​ d + 3 2 ​ d + 1 ​ N 6 ​ d 2 ​ d + 1 ) . O_{d}\left(u^{2}N^{\frac{3d}{d+1}}+u^{\frac{2d+3}{2d+1}}N^{\frac{6d}{2d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  H H  be the 3-uniform 3-partite hypergraph whose vertex sets are three copies of  P P  with an edge between a triple of vertices if they form an equilateral triangle. This is a semialgebraic hypergraph of total degree 4. It is defined by the equation 
 

 
 
 ( ( x 1 , x 2 ) , ( y 1 , y 2 ) , ( z 1 , z 2 ) ) ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) ⇔ ( x 1 − y 1 ) 2 + ( x 2 − y 2 ) 2 − ( x 1 − z 1 ) 2 − ( x 2 − z 2 ) 2 = 0  and  ​ ( x 1 − y 1 ) 2 + ( x 2 − y 2 ) 2 − ( y 1 − z 1 ) 2 − ( y 2 − z 2 ) 2 = 0 . \begin{split}((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}),(z_{1},z_{2}))\in E(H)&\iff(x_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(x_{2}-y_{2})^{2}-(x_{1}-z_{1})^{2}-(x_{2}-z_{2})^{2}=0\\
&\text{ and }(x_{1}-y_{1})^{2}+(x_{2}-y_{2})^{2}-(y_{1}-z_{1})^{2}-(y_{2}-z_{2})^{2}=0.\end{split} 
 
 
 
 
 Suppose for contradiction that  p 1 , … , p u , q 1 , … , q u , r 1 , … , r u ∈ P p_{1},\ldots,p_{u},q_{1},\ldots,q_{u},r_{1},\ldots,r_{u}\in P  form a copy of  K u , u , u ( 3 ) K_{u,u,u}^{(3)}  in  H H . By essentially the same argument as in  Proposition   6.1 , this implies that there exist mutually orthogonal affine subspaces  U , V , W U,V,W  all passing through the same point  v v  and  α , β , γ ≥ 0 \alpha,\beta,\gamma\geq 0  such that  α 2 + β 2 = α 2 + γ 2 = β 2 + γ 2 \alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=\alpha^{2}+\gamma^{2}=\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2}  so that  p 1 , … , p u p_{1},\ldots,p_{u}  lie on a sphere in  U U  of radius  α \alpha , so that  q 1 , … , q u q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}  lie on a sphere in  V V  of radius  β \beta , and so that  r 1 , … , r u r_{1},\ldots,r_{u}  lie on a sphere in  W W  of radius  γ \gamma . One of  U , V , W U,V,W  has dimension at most  ⌊ d / 3 ⌋ \left\lfloor d/3\right\rfloor , meaning that  u u  of these points lie on the same sphere of dimension at most  ⌊ d / 3 ⌋ − 1 \left\lfloor d/3\right\rfloor-1 , contradiction.
Therefore the desired bound follows from  Theorem   1.6  and the fact that  u ​ N 2 ≤ u 2 ​ d + 3 2 ​ d + 1 ​ N 6 ​ d 2 ​ d + 1 uN^{2}\leq u^{\frac{2d+3}{2d+1}}N^{\frac{6d}{2d+1}}  as  u , N ≥ 1 u,N\geq 1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 With more work, one can improve this bound to  O d ​ ( u 3 / ( 2 ​ d + 1 ) ​ N 6 ​ d / ( 2 ​ d + 1 ) ) O_{d}(u^{3/(2d+1)}N^{6d/(2d+1)}) . For  q 1 , … , q u ⊆ P q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}\subseteq P , let  H ′ = ( P ⊔ P , T ′ ) H^{\prime}=(P\sqcup P,T^{\prime})  be their common neighborhood, i.e.,  T ′ ⊆ P 2 T^{\prime}\subseteq P^{2}  is the set of pairs  ( p 1 , p 2 ) (p_{1},p_{2})  such that  ( p 1 , p 2 , q i ) ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) (p_{1},p_{2},q_{i})\in E(H)  for all  i ∈ [ u ] i\in[u] . In the proof of  Theorem   5.3 , we use the fact that  H ′ H^{\prime}  is a semialgebraic graph of total degree  O ⁡ ( u ) O(u) . In this case, one can check that  H ′ H^{\prime}  actually has total degree  O d ​ ( 1 ) O_{d}(1) . Now running through the proof of  Theorem   5.3  with this improved bound on the total degree of  H ′ H^{\prime}  gives the claimed bound on the number of equilateral triangles. 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.  The Erdős–Hajnal problem 

 
 The next two applications involve semialgebraic graphs and hypergraphs which are not partite. These are defined as follows. 
 
 
 We say that the formula  Φ ⁡ ( sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ) ) \Phi(\sgn(g_{1}(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd})),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd})))  is symmetric if for each  ( x 11 , … , x k ​ d ) ∈ ℝ k ​ d (x_{11},\ldots,x_{kd})\in\mathbb{R}^{kd}  the value of 
 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( sgn ⁡ ( g 1 ​ ( x π ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ 1 , … , x π ⁡ ( k ) ​ d ) ) , … , sgn ⁡ ( g t ​ ( x π ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ 1 , … , x π ⁡ ( k ) ​ d ) ) ) \Phi(\sgn(g_{1}(x_{\pi(1)1},\ldots,x_{\pi(k)d})),\ldots,\sgn(g_{t}(x_{\pi(1)1},\ldots,x_{\pi(k)d}))) 
 
 
 is independent of  π ∈ 𝔖 k \pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k} . Then we say that  H = ( V , E ) H=(V,E)  is a  k k -uniform semialgebraic hypergraph if there exists a symmetric  k k -uniform  k k -partite semialgebraic hypergraph  H ′ = ( V ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ V , E ′ ) H^{\prime}=(V\sqcup\cdots\sqcup V,E^{\prime})  such that  { v 1 , … , v k } \{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}  is an edge of  H H  if and only if  ( v 1 , … , v k ) (v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})  is an edge of  H ′ H^{\prime} . 
 
 
 By a quantitative version of Ramsey’s theorem proved by Erdős and Szekeres  [ 22 ] , every  N N -vertex graph has a clique or independent set of size  Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ N ) \Omega(\log N) . Erdős later proved that random graphs almost surely match this bound up to a constant multiplicative factor  [ 21 ] . However, it is believed that many restricted classes of graphs have significantly larger cliques or independent sets. We say that a family of graphs  ℱ \mathcal{F}  has the Erdős–Hajnal property if there exists some  c = c ⁡ ( ℱ ) > 0 c=c(\mathcal{F})>0  such that every  N N -vertex graph in  ℱ \mathcal{F}  contains a clique or independent set of size at least  N c N^{c} . The famous Erdős–Hajnal conjecture states that the family of induced  H H -free graphs has the Erdős–Hajnal property for every  H H . 
 
 
 In the paper which introduced semialgebraic graphs, Alon, Pach, Pinchasi, Radoičić, and Sharir proved that the class of semialgebraic graphs of bounded dimension and total degree has the Erdős–Hajnal property  [ 2 ] . More recent breakthroughs have shown that numerous other families have the Erdős–Hajnal property such as graphs of bounded VC-dimension  [ 42 ]  and induced  P 5 P_{5} -free graphs  [ 43 ] . From our semialgebraic Turán result we improve the quantitative bounds on the Erdős–Hajnal property for semialgebraic graphs, showing that such graphs contain a clique or independent set of size  N Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ D / d ​ log ⁡ D ) N^{\Omega(\log\log D/d\log D)} .
We also show that this exponent is tight up to a constant factor. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.4 . 
 
 Every  N N -vertex semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  contains a clique or independent set of size at least 
 

 
 
 N ( 1 2 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ D d ​ log ⁡ D N^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-o(1)\right)\frac{\log\log D}{d\log D}} 
 
 
 where the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1)  term goes to zero for each fixed  d d  as  D D  goes to infinity. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows via the same argument as the original proof of Alon, Pach, Pinchasi, Radoičić, and Sharir  [ 2 , Section 3.1] . We use our improved Turán result,  Corollary   1.5 , to improve the quantitative bounds. 
 
 
 Recall that the family of cographs is defined to be the minimal family of graphs that is closed under disjoint unions, complements, and contains  K 1 K_{1} . Fix  d , D d,D  and define  h ⁡ ( N ) h(N)  to be the largest integer  h h  such that any semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  and at least  N N  vertices contains a cograph of size at least  h h . Clearly  h ⁡ ( 1 ) = 1 h(1)=1 . 
 
 
 Now let  G G  be such a graph with  N ≥ 2 N\geq 2  vertices. Pick a bipartition  V ⁡ ( G ) = P ⊔ Q V(G)=P\sqcup Q  into parts of size  ⌊ N / 2 ⌋ , ⌈ N / 2 ⌉ \left\lfloor N/2\right\rfloor,\left\lceil N/2\right\rceil . Either  G G  or its complement contains at least  | P | ​ | Q | / 2 |P||Q|/2  edges in  P × Q P\times Q . We now apply  Corollary   1.5  to either  G ⁡ [ P × Q ] G[P\times Q]  or  G ¯ ​ [ P × Q ] \bar{G}[P\times Q]  with  ε = 1 / 2 \varepsilon=1/2 . Since both are semialgebraic bipartite graphs in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  of total degree  D D , we find  S ⊆ P S\subseteq P  and  T ⊆ Q T\subseteq Q  such that  S × T S\times T  is complete or empty in  G G  and such that  | S | ≥ N / ( C d ​ D d ) |S|\geq N/(C_{d}D^{d})  and  | T | ≥ N / C |T|\geq N/C . If  S ′ ⊆ S S^{\prime}\subseteq S  is a cograph in  G ⁡ [ S ] G[S]  and  T ′ ⊆ T T^{\prime}\subseteq T  is a cograph in  G ⁡ [ T ] G[T] , then  S ′ ⊔ T ′ S^{\prime}\sqcup T^{\prime}  is a cograph in  G G . Therefore we conclude that 
 

 
 
 h ⁡ ( N ) ≥ h ⁡ ( N C ) + h ⁡ ( N C d ​ D d ) h(N)\geq h\left(\frac{N}{C}\right)+h\left(\frac{N}{C_{d}D^{d}}\right) 
 
 
 holds for all  N > 1 N>1  for some constants  C , C d > 1 C,C_{d}>1 . 
 
 
 Given  N , D , d N,D,d , define  t t  to be the greatest positive integer so that 
 

 
 
 N ​ ( 1 C ) t ​ d ​ log ⁡ D / log ⁡ log ​ D ​ ( 1 C d ​ D d ) t ≥ 1 . N\left(\frac{1}{C}\right)^{td\log D/\log\log D}\left(\frac{1}{C_{d}D^{d}}\right)^{t}\geq 1. 
 
 
 Expanding out the previous recurrence, we see that 
 

 
 
 h ⁡ ( N ) ≥ ( ⌊ t ​ d ​ log ⁡ D / log ⁡ log ⁡ D ⌋ + t t ) ≥ ( d ​ log ⁡ D / log ⁡ log ⁡ D ) t . h(N)\geq\binom{\left\lfloor td\log D/\log\log D\right\rfloor+t}{t}\geq(d\log D/\log\log D)^{t}. 
 
 
 Since 
 

 
 
 t = ⌊ log ⁡ N log ⁡ ( C d ​ D d ) + ( d ​ log ⁡ D log ⁡ log ⁡ D ) ​ log ⁡ C ⌋ = ( 1 d ​ log ⁡ D − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ log ⁡ N , t=\left\lfloor\frac{\log N}{\log(C_{d}D^{d})+\left(\frac{d\log D}{\log\log D}\right)\log C}\right\rfloor=\left(\frac{1}{d\log D}-o(1)\right)\log N, 
 
 
 we conclude that 
 

 
 
 h ⁡ ( N ) ≳ N ( 1 d ​ log ⁡ D − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ ( log ⁡ log ⁡ D + log ⁡ d − log ⁡ log ⁡ log ⁡ D ) = N ( 1 − o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ D d ​ log ⁡ D . h(N)\gtrsim N^{\left(\frac{1}{d\log D}-o(1)\right)(\log\log D+\log d-\log\log\log D)}=N^{(1-o(1))\frac{\log\log D}{d\log D}}. 
 
 
 
 
 Finally, it is well known that every cograph is perfect and thus every  h h -vertex cograph contains a clique or independent set of size  h \sqrt{h} . Therefore we conclude that every  N N -vertex semialgebraic graph contains a clique or independent set of size at least  h ⁡ ( N ) \sqrt{h(N)} , as desired.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now construct semialgebraic graphs with no large cliques or independent sets. Taking the  r r -fold iterated blowup of a random graph of an appropriate size produces an  N N -vertex semialgebraic graph in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2}  of total degree  D D  with no clique or independent set of size  N ( 2 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ D / log ⁡ D N^{(2+o(1))\log\log D/\log D} . To give an efficient semialgebraic representation of this iterated blowup, we represent an  r r -tuple by viewing it as a single number written in base  B B . A similar base representation trick was earlier used in a construction of Conlon, Fox, Pach, Sudakov, and Suk  [ 15 , Section 3.4] . 
 
 
 To get the correct  d d -dependence in the exponent, we want to more efficiently represent this iterated blowup as a semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  as  d d  grows. We only know how to do this if the original graph that we blow up has an extra property. (See property  ( i ) (i)  in  Claim   6.6  below.) 
 
 
 Proposition 6.5 . 
 
 For  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  even, for  D D  sufficiently large in terms of  d d , and for  N ≥ D d N\geq D^{d} , there exists an  N N -vertex semialgebraic graph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  that does not contain a clique or independent set of size at least 
 

 
 
 N ( 4 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) ) ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ D d ​ log ⁡ D N^{\left(4+o(1)\right)\frac{\log\log D}{d\log D}} 
 
 
 where the  o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1)  term goes to zero for each fixed  d d  as  D D  goes to infinity. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First define  d = 2 ​ k d=2k . The graph we produce will be an iterated blowup of the graph supplied by the following claim. For  a , b ∈ S k a,b\in S^{k} , define  Φ ⁡ ( a , b ) ∈ ( S 2 ∪ { ∗ } ) k \Phi(a,b)\in(S^{2}\cup\{\ast\})^{k}  by  Φ i ​ ( a , b ) = ( a i , b i ) \Phi_{i}(a,b)=(a_{i},b_{i})  if  a i ≠ b i a_{i}\neq b_{i}  and  Φ i ( a i , b i ) = ∗ \Phi_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})=\ast  otherwise. 
 
 
 Claim 6.6 . 
 
 For a set  S S  of size  t ≥ 2 t\geq 2  and  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , there exists a graph  G = ( S k , E ) G=(S^{k},E)  with the following properties: 
 
 (i)  
 
 for vertices  a , b , c , d ∈ S k a,b,c,d\in S^{k}  with  Φ ⁡ ( a , b ) = Φ ⁡ ( c , d ) \Phi(a,b)=\Phi(c,d)  we have  { a , b } ∈ E \{a,b\}\in E  if and only if  { c , d } ∈ E \{c,d\}\in E ; and 
 
 (ii)  
 
 any clique or independent set in  G G  has size less than  O k ​ ( log ⁡ t ) O_{k}(\log t) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Choose a graph  G G  that satisfies  ( i ) (i)  uniformly at random. We will show by induction on  k k  that for any subset  U U  of  S k S^{k} , the probability that  G ⁡ [ U ] G[U]  is complete is upper bounded by  2 1 − Ω k ​ ( | U | 2 ) 2^{1-\Omega_{k}(\left\lvert U\right\rvert^{2})} . Consider a family of pairs  { a , b } \{a,b\} , such that  a i ≠ b i a_{i}\neq b_{i}  for all  i ∈ [ k ] i\in[k]  for each of these pairs. Note that each pair is an edge with probability  1 / 2 1/2  and these events are mutually independent. Therefore, if there are at least  | U | 2 / 4 − 1 \left\lvert U\right\rvert^{2}/4-1  pairs  { a , b } ∈ ( U 2 ) \{a,b\}\in\binom{U}{2}  with  a i ≠ b i a_{i}\neq b_{i}  for all  i ∈ [ k ] i\in[k] , then we are done. In particular, we are done if  k = 1 k=1 . 
 
 
 For the remaining case, there exists an  i ∈ [ k ] i\in[k]  such that there are at least  Ω k ​ ( | U | 2 ) \Omega_{k}(\left\lvert U\right\rvert^{2})  pairs  { a , b } ∈ ( U 2 ) \{a,b\}\in\binom{U}{2}  with  a i = b i a_{i}=b_{i} . Picking some  v ∈ U v\in U  that participates in  Ω k ​ ( | U | ) \Omega_{k}(|U|)  of these pairs, we get that the set  U ′ = { a ∈ U : a i = v i } U^{\prime}=\{a\in U:a_{i}=v_{i}\}  has size at least  Ω k ​ ( | U | ) \Omega_{k}(\left\lvert U\right\rvert) . Note crucially that  G ⁡ [ U ′ ] G[U^{\prime}]  has the same distribution as the graph constructed the same way on  S k − 1 S^{k-1} . Therefore by the inductive hypothesis

 6.3.  Property testing 

 
 Property testing is an area of theoretical computer science that aims to find extremely fast randomized algorithms that determine if an object has some property or is far from satisfying the property. Typically the goal is to find an algorithm that only makes a constant number of queries to the object. In the dense graph model one can query if a pair of vertices form an edge or not. We say that a property  𝒫 \mathcal{P}  is testable if for all  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0  there exists an algorithm that makes  O ε ​ ( 1 ) O_{\varepsilon}(1)  queries to a graph  G G  and, if  G ∈ 𝒫 G\in\mathcal{P} , then the algorithm accepts, and if  G G  is  ε \varepsilon -far from  𝒫 \mathcal{P} , then the algorithm rejects with probability at least 2/3. We say that an  N N -vertex graph is  ε \varepsilon -close to  𝒫 \mathcal{P}  if by adding or removing at most  ε ​ N 2 \varepsilon N^{2}  edges,  G G  can be turned into an element of  𝒫 \mathcal{P} ; otherwise  G G  is  ε \varepsilon -far from  𝒫 \mathcal{P} . 
 
 
 Alon and Shapira classified the testable graph properties as exactly those that satisfy a combinatorial property known as being semi-hereditary  [ 4 ] . This includes monotone properties, such as  H H -freeness, as well as hereditary properties, such as induced  H H -freeness, as well as many additional properties which may be more pathological. The strength of this result lies in the fact that the number of queries is independent of the size of the graph. However, it can an extremely large function of  ε \varepsilon . For properties such as  H H -freeness and induced  H H -freeness the number of queries has tower-type growth as a function of  ε \varepsilon . For arbitrary semi-hereditary properties, the number of queries can grow essentially arbitrarily quickly for some pathological properties. 
 
 
 When restricted to the class of semialgebraic graphs and hypergraphs of bounded dimension and total degree, the situation is quantitatively nicer. Properties such as  H H -freeness and induced  H H -freeness can be tested with  ε − O ⁡ ( 1 ) \varepsilon^{-O(1)}  queries while general hereditary properties can be testing with a number of queries that is polynomial in a function that captures the structure of the property  [ 25 ] . These results also generalize exactly to hypergraphs. All of these results follow from Fox, Pach, and Suk’s regularity lemma for semialgebraic hypergraphs. With our quantitatively improved regularity lemma, we can improve the number of queries required. For simplicity, we only carry out this analysis for  H H -freeness, though we expect our results to be able to say something about the more general problem. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.7 . 
 
 Let  H H  be a  k k -uniform hypergraph. In the class of  k k -uniform semialgebraic hypergraphs in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree at most  D D , the property of  H H -freeness can be tested with  O d , k , | H | ​ ( ε − ( d + 1 ) ​ | H | ​ D d ​ | H | ) O_{d,k,|H|}(\varepsilon^{-(d+1)|H|}D^{d|H|})  queries. 
 
 
 
 There is a close relationship between property testing results and removal lemmas. Via standard arguments, the above proposition follows from (and is essentially equivalent to) the upcoming removal lemma. The property testing algorithm is very simple. Sample  O d , k , | H | ​ ( ε − ( d + 1 ) ​ | H | ​ D d ​ | H | ) O_{d,k,|H|}(\varepsilon^{-(d+1)|H|}D^{d|H|})  random injections from  H H  into the target hypergraph  G G  and check if any of them correspond to a copy of  H H  in  G G . If any do, the algorithm rejects; otherwise it accepts. The following removal lemma shows the correctness of this algorithm. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.8 . 
 
 For  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , there exists  δ = Ω d , k , n ​ ( ε ( d + 1 ) ​ n ​ D − d ​ n ) \delta=\Omega_{d,k,n}(\varepsilon^{(d+1)n}D^{-dn})  such that the following holds. Let  H H  be an  n n -vertex  k k -uniform hypergraph and let  G G  be an  N N -vertex  k k -uniform semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  of total degree at most  D D . If  G G  has fewer than  δ ​ N n \delta N^{n}  labelled copies of  H H , then  G G  can be made  H H -free by removing at most  ε ​ N k \varepsilon N^{k}  edges. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) . We apply multilevel polynomial partitioning to  V V  with parameter  A = C d , k ​ D / ε A=C_{d,k}D/\varepsilon . This produces a partition  Π \Pi  of  V V  with  O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) O_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d})  parts. By the same argument as in the proof of  Theorem   1.3 , all but an  ( ε / 2 ) (\varepsilon/2) -fraction of  k k -tuples of vertices lie in a  k k -tuple of parts which is homogeneous. (To be clear, we say that a  k k -tuple of not necessarily distinct parts  ( π 1 , … , π k ) (\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})  is complete if every  k k -tuples of vertices  ( v 1 , … , v k ) ∈ π 1 × ⋯ × π k (v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})\in\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k}  for which  v 1 , … , v k v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}  are distinct is an edge and the analogous definition for an empty  k k -tuple.) 
 
 
 Now we define a “cleaned up” hypergraph  G ~ \tilde{G}  on the same vertex set of  G G . We produce  G ~ \tilde{G}  by first removing any edge that lies an a  k k -tuple of parts that is not homogeneous. We also remove any edge that lies in a  k k -tuple of parts  ( π 1 , … , π k ) (\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})  where any of the parts have size less than  ε ​ N / ( 2 ​ | Π | ​ k ) \varepsilon N/(2|\Pi|k) . In total we have removed at most  ε ​ N k \varepsilon N^{k}  edges of  G G . 
 
 
 We prove the contrapositive. Suppose that  G G  cannot be made  H H -free by removing at most  ε ​ N k \varepsilon N^{k}  edges. Then  G ~ \tilde{G}  must contain a copy of  H H , say between parts  π 1 , … , π n \pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n} . By the construction of  G ~ \tilde{G} , we know that if vertex  i i  of  H H  is non-isolated then  | π i | ≥ ε ​ N / ( 2 ​ | Π | ​ k ) |\pi_{i}|\geq\varepsilon N/(2|\Pi|k)  and for each edge  { i 1 , … , i k } \{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}  of  H H , the tuple  ( π i 1 , … , π i k ) (\pi_{i_{1}},\ldots,\pi_{i_{k}})  is complete in  G G . Now we can lower bound the number of labelled copies of  H H  in  G G  by the number of copies where each non-isolated vertex of  H H  maps into the corresponding part of the partition (and the isolated vertices map into arbitrary parts). This number is at least 
 

 
 
 ( ε ​ N 2 ​ | Π | ​ k ) ( ε ​ N 2 ​ | Π | ​ k − 1 ) ⋯ ( ε ​ N 2 ​ | Π | ​ k − ( n − 1 ) ) ≥ δ N n . \left(\frac{\varepsilon N}{2|\Pi|k}\right)\left(\frac{\varepsilon N}{2|\Pi|k}-1\right)\cdots\left(\frac{\varepsilon N}{2|\Pi|k}-(n-1)\right)\geq\delta N^{n}. 
 
 
 The last inequality holds for an appropriate  δ = Ω d , k , n ​ ( ε ( d + 1 ) ​ n ​ D − d ​ n ) \delta=\Omega_{d,k,n}(\varepsilon^{(d+1)n}D^{-dn})  if  N ≥ N 0 N\geq N_{0}  where we define  N 0 = 4 ​ k ​ n ​ | Π | ​ ε − 1 N_{0}=4kn|\Pi|\varepsilon^{-1} . For  N < N 0 N<N_{0} , the desired statement holds for  δ = N 0 − n \delta=N_{0}^{-n}  for all  ε \varepsilon , completing the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.  Lower bound constructions 

 
 We give three examples of semialgebraic graphs of increasing complexity. The first shows that the regularity lemma,  Theorem   1.3 , has optimal  ε \varepsilon -dependence, while the third shows that the dependence on both  ε \varepsilon  and  D D  is optimal for all  d d . 
 
 
 Example 7.1 . 
 
 For every  m , d ≥ 1 m,d\geq 1 , let  P 1 = P 2 = [ m ] d P_{1}=P_{2}=[m]^{d} . Define the graph  G = ( P 1 ⊔ P 2 , E ) G=(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2},E)  where  x x  is adjacent to  y y  if  x i = y i x_{i}=y_{i}  for some  i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d] . This graph is semialgebraic in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  of total degree  d d . 
 
 
 
 For any  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0  and  m m  sufficiently large in terms of  d , ε d,\varepsilon , we will show that any homogeneous partition of the above graph with error  ε \varepsilon  must have  Ω d ​ ( ε − d ) \Omega_{d}(\varepsilon^{-d})  parts. This shows that the  ε \varepsilon -dependence in  Theorem   1.3  is optimal. 
 
 
 For our next examples, for each  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  we define real numbers  a 0 , … , a k − 1 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) a_{0},\ldots,a_{k-1}\in[0,1)  as follows. Let  p p  be the smallest prime greater than  k k . Then define  a i ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) a_{i}\in[0,1)  so that  i 2 / p ≡ a i ( mod 1 ) i^{2}/p\equiv a_{i}\pmod{1} . 
 
 
 Example 7.2 . 
 
 For  D , m ≥ 1 D,m\geq 1 , let  a 0 , … , a D − 1 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) a_{0},\ldots,a_{D-1}\in[0,1)  be the real numbers defined above. Set  P 1 = [ m ] P_{1}=[m]  and  P 2 = [ 2 ​ m ] P_{2}=[2m] . Define the graph  G = ( P 1 ⊔ P 2 , E ) G=(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2},E)  where  x x  is adjacent to  y y  if an even number of the inequalities 
 

 
 
 y − x − i ​ m D − a i ​ m 3 ​ D ≥ 0 for  ​ 0 ≤ i < D y-x-\frac{im}{D}-\frac{a_{i}m}{3D}\geq 0\qquad\text{for }0\leq i<D 
 
 
 hold. This graph is semialgebraic in  ℝ 1 \mathbb{R}^{1}  of total degree  D D . 
 
 
 
 The edges in  G G  correspond to pairs  ( x , y ) (x,y)  lying in the black region in the following diagram. 
 
 
 
 
 For any  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , any  D ≥ 1 D\geq 1 , and any  m m  sufficiently large in terms of  D , ε D,\varepsilon , we will show that any homogeneous partition of the above graph with error  ε \varepsilon  must have  Ω ⁡ ( D / ε ) \Omega(D/\varepsilon)  parts, showing that the  ε \varepsilon - and the  D D -dependence in  Theorem   1.3  are both optimal when  d = 1 d=1 . This also generalizes to higher dimensions. 
 
 
 Example 7.3 . 
 
 For  D , m , d ≥ 1 D,m,d\geq 1 , set  k = ⌊ D / d ⌋ k=\left\lfloor D/d\right\rfloor . Let  a 0 , … , a k − 1 ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) a_{0},\ldots,a_{k-1}\in[0,1)  be the real numbers defined above. Set  P 1 = [ m ] d P_{1}=[m]^{d}  and  P 2 = [ 2 ​ m ] d P_{2}=[2m]^{d} . Define the graph  G = ( P 1 ⊔ P 2 , E ) G=(P_{1}\sqcup P_{2},E)  where  x x  is adjacent to  y y  if an even number of the inequalities 
 

 
 
 y j − x j − i ​ m k − a i ​ m 3 ​ k ≥ 0 for  ​ 0 ≤ i < k ​  and  ​ j ∈ [ d ] y_{j}-x_{j}-\frac{im}{k}-\frac{a_{i}m}{3k}\geq 0\qquad\text{for }0\leq i<k\text{ and }j\in[d] 
 
 
 hold. This graph is semialgebraic in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  of total degree  k ​ d ≤ D kd\leq D . This graph can either be written in terms of  k ​ d kd  polynomials of degree 1 or in terms of a single polynomial of degree  k ​ d kd . 
 
 
 
 For any  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , any  D ≥ 2 ​ d D\geq 2d , and any  m m  sufficiently large in terms of  D , ε , d D,\varepsilon,d , we will show that any homogeneous partition of the above graph with error  ε \varepsilon  must have  Ω d ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) \Omega_{d}((D/\varepsilon)^{d})  parts, showing that both the  ε \varepsilon - and the  D D -dependencies in  Theorem   1.3  are optimal. 
 
 
 Proposition 7.4 . 
 
 For every  d ≥ 1 d\geq 1  and every  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , for  m m  sufficiently large, the graph in  Example   7.1  has the property that any homogeneous partition with error  ε \varepsilon  must have at least  ( 2 ​ ε ) − d (2\varepsilon)^{-d}  parts. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose  m ≥ d ​ ε − ( d + 1 ) m\geq d\varepsilon^{-(d+1)} . Let  G G  be the graph in  Example   7.1 . For a set  A ⊆ P 1 A\subseteq P_{1} , write  N ⁡ ( A ) N(A)  for the neighborhood of  A A  in  G G , i.e., the set of vertices in  P 2 P_{2}  that are adjacent to at least one vertex in  A A . Also write  π j ​ ( A ) ⊆ [ m ] \pi_{j}(A)\subseteq[m]  for the projection of  A A  onto the  j j th coordinate axis, i.e., the set of  a ∈ [ m ] a\in[m]  such that there exists  x ∈ A x\in A  with  x j = a x_{j}=a . 
 
 
 Claim 7.5 . 
 
 For  A ⊆ P 1 A\subseteq P_{1} , 
 

 
 
 | N ⁡ ( A ) | ≥ m d − ( m − | A | 1 / d ) d ≥ | A | 1 / d ​ m d − 1 . |N(A)|\geq m^{d}-(m-|A|^{1/d})^{d}\geq|A|^{1/d}m^{d-1}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For  y ∈ P 2 y\in P_{2} , we have  y ∉ N ⁡ ( A ) y\not\in N(A)  if and only if  y j ∉ π j ​ ( A ) y_{j}\not\in\pi_{j}(A)  for all  j j . Thus we see that

 by two applications of the AM-GM inequality. 
 
 
 Since  A ⊆ π 1 ( A ) × ⋯ × π d ( A ) A\subseteq\pi_{1}(A)\times\cdots\times\pi_{d}(A) , we see that  ∏ j = 1 d | π j ​ ( A ) | ≥ | A | \prod_{j=1}^{d}|\pi_{j}(A)|\geq|A| , so  | N ⁡ ( A ) | ≥ m d − ( m − | A | 1 / d ) d |N(A)|\geq m^{d}-(m-|A|^{1/d})^{d} .  1 − | A | 1 / d m ≥ ( 1 − | A | 1 / d m ) d 1-\frac{|A|^{1/d}}{m}\geq\left(1-\frac{|A|^{1/d}}{m}\right)^{d} 
 
 
 since the left-hand side is at most 1 and  d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 . Multiplying by  m d m^{d}  and rearranging gives the desired inequality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Suppose for contradiction that  P 1 = A 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ A K P_{1}=A_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup A_{K}  and  P 2 = B 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B K P_{2}=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{K}  are partitions with  K < ( 2 ​ ε ) − d K<(2\varepsilon)^{-d}  parts that form a homogeneous partition of  G G  with error  ε \varepsilon .  ∑ i , j : E ⁡ [ A i × B j ] ​  non-empty | A i | ​ | B j | m 2 ​ d ≤ ε + K 2 ​ d 2 m 2 ≤ 2 ε , \sum_{i,j:E[A_{i}\times B_{j}]\text{ non-empty}}\frac{|A_{i}||B_{j}|}{m^{2d}}\leq\varepsilon+\frac{K^{2}d^{2}}{m^{2}}\leq 2\varepsilon, 
 
 
 where the last inequality follows from the assumption that  m m  is sufficiently large.
Furthermore, we can bound 
 

 
 
 ∑ i , j : E ⁡ [ A i × B j ] ​  non-empty | A i | ​ | B j | m 2 ​ d \displaystyle\sum_{i,j:E[A_{i}\times B_{j}]\text{ non-empty}}\frac{|A_{i}||B_{j}|}{m^{2d}} 
 ≥ ∑ i = 1 K | A i | ​ | N ⁡ ( A i ) | m 2 ​ d \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{|A_{i}||N(A_{i})|}{m^{2d}} 
 
 
 [ Claim   7.5 ] 
 
 
 ≥ ∑ i = 1 K | A i | 1 + 1 d ​ m d − 1 m 2 ​ d \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{|A_{i}|^{1+\tfrac{1}{d}}m^{d-1}}{m^{2d}} 
 
 
 
 
 = K m d + 1 ​ ( 1 K ​ ∑ i = 1 K | A i | 1 + 1 d ) \displaystyle=\frac{K}{m^{d+1}}\left(\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}|A_{i}|^{1+\tfrac{1}{d}}\right) 
 
 
 [Jensen’s inequality] 
 
 
 ≥ K m d + 1 ​ ( 1 K ​ ∑ i = 1 K | A i | ) 1 + 1 d \displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{m^{d+1}}\left(\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}|A_{i}|\right)^{1+\tfrac{1}{d}}

 Combining this lower bound with the previous upper bound, we conclude that  K ≥ ( 2 ​ ε ) − d K\geq(2\varepsilon)^{-d} , as desired.

 We see that understanding the behavior of neighborhoods plays an important role in the proof.
In the proof that  Example   7.3  does not have small homogeneous partitions, we will show that neighborhoods of far-away points differ a lot, which will again be key in the proof. 
 
 
 Proposition 7.6 . 
 
 For every  D ≥ 2 ​ d D\geq 2d  and every  ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 / 1000 ) \varepsilon\in(0,1/1000) , for  m m  sufficiently large, the graph in  Example   7.3  has the property that any homogeneous partition with error  ε \varepsilon  must have at least  ( D / ( 32 ​ ε ​ d ) ) d (D/(32\varepsilon d))^{d}  parts.

 Claim 7.7 . 
 
 For each  1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k 1\leq\ell\leq k , the number of pairs  ( i , i ′ ) ∈ { 0 , 1 ​ … , k − ℓ − 1 } 2 (i,i^{\prime})\in\{0,1\ldots,k-\ell-1\}^{2}  with  i ≠ i ′ i\neq i^{\prime}  and satisfying

 is at most  k 2 / 4 − k / 2 k^{2}/4-k/2 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This is easy to check for  k = 2 , 3 k=2,3 . Otherwise, we have  k < p < 2 ​ k − 2 k<p<2k-2  since  p p  is the smallest prime greater than  k k . Now if  ( i , i ′ ) (i,i^{\prime})  satisfies the above condition, then

 is within  1 / 16 1/16  of an integer. Since  2 ​ ℓ ≢ 0 ( mod p ) 2\ell\not\equiv 0\pmod{p} , this implies that there are at most  2 ​ ⌊ p / 16 ⌋ 2\left\lfloor p/16\right\rfloor  possible values of  i − i ′ ≠ 0 i-i^{\prime}\neq 0  and thus at most  k ⁡ ( 2 ​ ⌊ p / 16 ⌋ ) ≤ k ⁡ ( p − 1 ) / 8 ≤ k ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 4 ) / 8 = k 2 / 4 − k / 2 k(2\left\lfloor p/16\right\rfloor)\leq k(p-1)/8\leq k(2k-4)/8=k^{2}/4-k/2  possible values for  ( i , i ′ ) (i,i^{\prime}) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 We next show that vertices that are far apart have very different neighborhoods in  H H  and also that these neighborhoods are not close to complementary. 
 
 
 Claim 7.8 . 
 
 For  x , x ′ ∈ [ m ] x,x^{\prime}\in[m] , 
 

 
 
 | N H ​ ( x ) ​ Δ ​ N H ​ ( x ′ ) | , 2 ​ m − | N H ​ ( x ) ​ Δ ​ N H ​ ( x ′ ) | ≥ min ⁡ { k ⁡ ( | x − x ′ | − 1 ) 2 , m 200 } . |N_{H}(x)\Delta N_{H}(x^{\prime})|,2m-|N_{H}(x)\Delta N_{H}(x^{\prime})|\geq\min\left\{\frac{k(|x-x^{\prime}|-1)}{2},\frac{m}{200}\right\}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Define the two sets

 and

 Since  a i ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) a_{i}\in[0,1) , the elements of  S S  are each separated by at least  2 ​ m / 3 ​ k 2m/3k . The same holds for the elements of  T T . For such a pair  ( i , i ′ ) (i,i^{\prime}) , we have 
 

 
 (8) 
 
 | ( x + i ​ m k + a i ​ m 3 ​ k ) − ( x ′ + i ′ ​ m k + a i ′ ​ m 3 ​ k ) | < min ⁡ { | x − x ′ | , m 96 ​ k } . \left\lvert\left(x+\frac{im}{k}+\frac{a_{i}m}{3k}\right)-\left(x^{\prime}+\frac{i^{\prime}m}{k}+\frac{a_{i^{\prime}}m}{3k}\right)\right\rvert<\min\left\{|x-x^{\prime}|,\frac{m}{96k}\right\}. 
 
 
 Note that if  i = i ′ i=i^{\prime} , the above inequality would imply that  | x − x ′ | < | x − x ′ | |x-x^{\prime}|<|x-x^{\prime}|  which does not hold. Thus we have  i ≠ i ′ i\neq i^{\prime} .

 If  ( i , i + ℓ ) (i,i+\ell)  and  ( i ′ , i ′ + ℓ ) (i^{\prime},i^{\prime}+\ell)  both satisfy  8 , we then have

 Therefore by  Claim   7.7 , we see that there are at most  k / 2 k/2  pairs of elements of  S ⊔ T S\sqcup T  that are at distance smaller than  min ⁡ { | x − x ′ | , m / ( 96 ​ k ) } \min\{|x-x^{\prime}|,m/(96k)\} .

Therefore we have shown that 
 

 
 
 | N H ​ ( x ) ​ Δ ​ N H ​ ( x ′ ) | , 2 ​ m − | N H ​ ( x ) ​ Δ ​ N H ​ ( x ′ ) | ≥ k 2 ​ ( min ⁡ { | x − x ′ | , m 96 ​ k } − 1 ) . |N_{H}(x)\Delta N_{H}(x^{\prime})|,2m-|N_{H}(x)\Delta N_{H}(x^{\prime})|\geq\frac{k}{2}\left(\min\left\{|x-x^{\prime}|,\frac{m}{96k}\right\}-1\right). 
 
 
 This proves the desired inequality since  m ≥ 4000 ​ k m\geq 4000k .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now we claim that for  x , x ′ ∈ [ m ] d x,x^{\prime}\in[m]^{d}  we have 
 

 
 (9) 
 
 | N G ​ ( x ) ​ Δ ​ N G ​ ( x ′ ) | ≥ ( 2 ​ m ) d − 1 ⋅ min ⁡ { k ⁡ ( ‖ x − x ′ ‖ ∞ − 1 ) 2 , m 200 } . |N_{G}(x)\Delta N_{G}(x^{\prime})|\geq(2m)^{d-1}\cdot\min\left\{\frac{k(\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}-1)}{2},\frac{m}{200}\right\}. 
 
 
 To see this, fix  j ∈ [ d ] j\in[d]  such that  | x j − x j ′ | = ‖ x − x ′ ‖ ∞ |x_{j}-x^{\prime}_{j}|=\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty} . For any  z ∈ [ 2 ​ m ] [ d ] ∖ { j } z\in[2m]^{[d]\setminus\{j\}} , consider the line  L z = { y ∈ [ 2 ​ m ] d : y i = z i ​  for all  ​ i ∈ [ d ] ∖ { j } } L_{z}=\{y\in[2m]^{d}:y_{i}=z_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[d]\setminus\{j\}\} . Clearly these  ( 2 ​ m ) d − 1 (2m)^{d-1}  lines partition  P 2 P_{2} .  To complete the proof, suppose for contradiction that  P 1 = A 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ A K P_{1}=A_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup A_{K}  and  P 2 = B 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ B K P_{2}=B_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup B_{K}  are partitions with  K ≤ ( D / ( 32 ​ d ​ ε ) ) − d K\leq(D/(32d\varepsilon))^{-d}  parts that form a homogeneous partition of  G G  with error  ε \varepsilon . Pick points  x i , x i ′ ∈ A i x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}  that achieve  max x i , x i ′ ∈ A i ⁡ ‖ x i − x i ′ ‖ ∞ \max_{x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\in A_{i}}\|x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i}\|_{\infty} . Note that  | A i | ≤ ( ‖ x i ′ − x i ‖ ∞ + 1 ) d |A_{i}|\leq(\|x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i}\|_{\infty}+1)^{d}  and also for any part  B j B_{j}  with  y ∈ B j y\in B_{j}  such that  y ∈ N G ​ ( x i ) ​ Δ ​ N G ​ ( x i ′ ) y\in N_{G}(x_{i})\Delta N_{G}(x^{\prime}_{i}) , we know  E ⁡ [ A i × B j ] E[A_{i}\times B_{j}]  is not homogeneous. Thus 
 

 
 
 ε \displaystyle\varepsilon 
 ≥ ∑ i , j : E ⁡ [ A i × B j ] ​  not homogeneous | A i | ​ | B j | m d ​ ( 2 ​ m ) d \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i,j:E[A_{i}\times B_{j}]\text{ not homogeneous}}\frac{|A_{i}||B_{j}|}{m^{d}(2m)^{d}} 
 
 
 
 
 ≥ ∑ i | A i | ​ | N G ​ ( x i ) ​ Δ ​ N G ​ ( x i ′ ) | m d ​ ( 2 ​ m ) d \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\frac{|A_{i}||N_{G}(x_{i})\Delta N_{G}(x^{\prime}_{i})|}{m^{d}(2m)^{d}} 
 
 
 [by  9 ] 
 
 
 ≥ ∑ i min ⁡ { ( 2 ​ m ) d − 1 ​ | A i | ​ k ​ ( ‖ x i ′ − x i ‖ ∞ − 1 ) 2 ​ m d ​ ( 2 ​ m ) d , ( 2 ​ m ) d − 1 ​ | A i | ​ m 200 ​ m d ​ ( 2 ​ m ) d } \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\min\left\{\frac{(2m)^{d-1}|A_{i}|k(\|x^{\prime}_{i}-x_{i}\|_{\infty}-1)}{2m^{d}(2m)^{d}},\frac{(2m)^{d-1}|A_{i}|m}{200m^{d}(2m)^{d}}\right\}

 Since  k ≥ D / ( 2 ​ d ) k\geq D/(2d) , this rearranges to the desired result.

 8.  Open problems 

 
 Our regularity lemma produces partitions with  O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) O_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d})  parts. Tracing through the dependencies, the hidden constant is  exp ⁡ ( ( d ​ k ) O ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \exp\left((dk)^{O(1)}\right) . In contrast we prove a lower bound of  ( 32 ​ d ) − d (32d)^{-d}  on this constant. It seems likely that the dependence on dimension can be improved in some of the polynomial method tools we use from  [ 56 ]  which would start to close this gap. 
 
 
 Problem 8.1 . 
 
 Improve the dependence of the number of parts on  d d  and  k k  in the regularity lemma,  Theorem   1.3 . 
 
 
 
 To deduce the equitable version of the regularity lemma, we lose a factor of  ε \varepsilon . In dimension  d = 1 d=1 , we can produce the equitable partitions by hand with no loss. It would be interesting if one could avoid this loss in all dimensions. 
 
 
 Conjecture 8.2 . 
 
 Corollary   1.4  holds with partitions into  O d , k ​ ( ( D / ε ) d ) O_{d,k}((D/\varepsilon)^{d})  parts. 
 
 
 
 We do not know if either our general Turán and Zarankiewicz results are tight except for a very small number of examples. 
 
 
 Question 8.3 . 
 
 What is the correct exponent of  ε \varepsilon  in the semialgebraic Turán result,  Corollary   1.5 ? 
 
 
 
 It is easy to see that  Corollary   1.5  is tight in dimension  d = 1 d=1 . However, we do not know if it is tight in any other dimension. 
 
 
 For  d = 2 d=2 , a tight example for the Szemerédi–Trotter theorem gives a semialgebraic graph  G 0 G_{0}  with  n n  vertices at  C ​ n 4 / 3 Cn^{4/3}  edges. This graph is  K 2 , 2 K_{2,2} -free. Let  G G  be the  r r -fold blowup of  G 0 G_{0} . This is an  n ​ r nr -vertex graph with  C ​ n 4 / 3 ​ r 2 Cn^{4/3}r^{2}  edges. The graph  G G  is still semialgebraic in  ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2}  of total degree  O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) . Furthermore, for any complete  S × T S\times T  in  G G , we see that  min ⁡ { | S | , | T | } ≤ r \min\{|S|,|T|\}\leq r . Since  G G  has edge density  ε = C n − 2 / 3 \varepsilon=Cn^{-2/3} , this shows that the exponent of 2 in the Turán bound  | S | = Ω d ​ ( ε 2 ​ N ) |S|=\Omega_{d}(\varepsilon^{2}N)  and  | T | = Ω ⁡ ( ε ​ N ) |T|=\Omega(\varepsilon N)  cannot be replaced by anything smaller than  3 / 2 3/2 . 
 
 
 For the Zarankiewicz problem, the Szemerédi–Trotter example shows that the power of  N N  is correct in dimension 2. However for dimensions 3 and higher, the best-known lower bounds are the lower bounds for the point-hyperplane incidence problem  [ 51 ]  which do not match the upper bound. 
 
 
 Question 8.4 . 
 
 What is the correct exponent of  N N  in the semialgebraic Zarankiewicz result,  Theorem   1.6 ? 
 
 
 
 One can also ask for the correct exponents of  u u  and  D D . When  k = 2 k=2 , the bound in  Theorem   1.6  is  O d ​ ( u 2 / ( d + 1 ) ​ D 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) ​ N 2 ​ d / ( d + 1 ) ) O_{d}(u^{2/(d+1)}D^{2d/(d+1)}N^{2d/(d+1)}) . We can say at least that neither of the exponents on  u u  or  N N  can be decreased without increasing the other, since for  u = N u=N  and  D = 1 D=1 ,  Theorem   1.6  gives the bound  | E | = O d ​ ( N 2 ) |E|=O_{d}(N^{2}) , which is tight for these parameters. (The bound is tight in the sense that it cannot be improved by  N c N^{c}  for any  c > 0 c>0  which is independent of  u u .)
Moreover, as every  N N -vertex graph is semialgebraic in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  of total degree  O d ​ ( N 1 / d ) O_{d}(N^{1/d})  we can also say that, for  k = 2 k=2 , neither of the exponents on  D D  or  N N  can be decreased without increasing the other, since for  k = 2 k=2  and  u ∼ 1 u\sim 1  and  D ∼ N 1 / d D\sim N^{1/d} ,  Theorem   1.6  gives the bound  | E | = O d ​ ( N 2 ) |E|=O_{d}(N^{2}) , which is tight. We conjecture that for  k > 2 k>2 , the exponents on  u u  and  D D  can be decreased so that the bound is tight in this way. 
 
 
 Conjecture 8.5 . 
 
 Let  H H  be a  K u , … , u ( k ) K^{(k)}_{u,\ldots,u} -free semialgebraic hypergraph in  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}  with total degree  D D  and parts of size  N N . Then the number of edges of  H H  is at most 
 

 
 
 O d , k ​ ( u k ( k − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ D k ​ d ( k − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ​ N k − k ( k − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) . O_{d,k}\left(u^{\frac{k}{(k-1)d+1}}D^{\frac{kd}{(k-1)d+1}}N^{k-\frac{k}{(k-1)d+1}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 For semialgebraic hypergraphs with a particularly simple structure, one can prove the bound  O D , d , k ​ ( u k / ( ( k − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) ​ N k − k / ( ( k − 1 ) ​ d + 1 ) ) O_{D,d,k}(u^{k/((k-1)d+1)}N^{k-k/((k-1)d+1)})  by modifying the proof of  Theorem   5.3 . For example, suppose that there exists a single polynomial  g g  such that  ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ P 1 × ⋯ × P k (x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{k}  is an edge of  H H  if and only if  g ⁡ ( x 1 , … , x k ) = 0 g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=0 . In the proof of  Theorem   5.3 , we considered the  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -uniform hypergraph  H ′ = ( P 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P k − 1 , T ′ ) H^{\prime}=(P_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{k-1},T^{\prime})  which is the common neighborhood in  H H  of vertices  q 1 , … , q u ∈ P k q_{1},\ldots,q_{u}\in P_{k} . In that proof, we used the fact that  H ′ H^{\prime}  is a semialgebraic hypergraph of total degree  u ​ D uD . If  H H  has the above simple form, then instead  H ′ H^{\prime}  has total degree  O D , d , k ​ ( 1 ) O_{D,d,k}(1) , with no  u u -dependence. (This is a generalization of the observation in the remark after  Proposition   6.3 .) This shows that we can prove the conjectured  u u -dependence at the cost of significantly worse  D D -dependence for these particularly simple semialgebraic hypergraphs. 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  Deduction of polynomial method results 

 
 A.1.  Proof of  Theorem   2.2 

 
 For a variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  and a point  x ∈ V ⁡ ( ℝ ) x\in V(\mathbb{R}) , we write  dim x ℝ ( V ) \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{R}}(V)  for the local real dimension of  V ⁡ ( ℝ ) V(\mathbb{R})  at  x x .
Clearly  dim x ℝ ( V ) ≤ dim x ℂ ( V ) \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{R}}(V)\leq\dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)  for every  x ∈ V ⁡ ( ℝ ) x\in V(\mathbb{R}) .
We will need the following definitions that are made in  [ 56 , Section 7.1] . 
 
 
 Definition A.1 . 
 
 Let  Q = ( Q 1 , Q 2 , … , Q m ) Q=(Q_{1},Q_{2},\ldots,Q_{m})  be a sequence of polynomials in some ambient space  ℂ d \mathbb{C}^{d} .
For any  0 ≤ j ≤ m 0\leq j\leq m , define  Z j ​ ( Q ) Z_{j}(Q)  to be  Z ⁡ ( Q 1 , … , Q j ) Z(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{j}) , and for every  x ∈ ℝ d x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}  let  dim Q , ( j ) ℝ ( x ) \dim^{\mathbb{R}}_{Q,(j)}(x)  be the  j j -tuple recording the local real dimensions  ( dim x ℝ Z 1 ​ ( Q ) , … , dim x ℝ Z j ​ ( Q ) ) (\dim^{\mathbb{R}}_{x}Z_{1}(Q),\ldots,\dim^{\mathbb{R}}_{x}Z_{j}(Q)) .
Lastly, for any  j j -tuple of non-negative integers  τ \tau , define  Z τ ​ ( Q ) Z_{\tau}(Q)  to be the set 
 

 
 
 { x ∈ ℝ d : dim Q , ( j ) ℝ ( x ) = τ } ¯ \overline{\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\dim^{\mathbb{R}}_{Q,(j)}(x)=\tau\}} 
 
 
 where the closure is in the Euclidean topology on  ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . 
 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   2.2 . 
 
 We first reorder  Q 1 , … , Q d − r Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{d-r}  so that their degrees are non-decreasing.
Now for every  x ∈ Z ​ ( Q ) ​ ( ℝ ) x\in Z(Q)(\mathbb{R})  with  dim x ℂ Z ⁡ ( Q ) = r \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}Z(Q)=r , by Krull’s theorem, we must have that  dim x ℂ Z j ​ ( Q ) = d − j \dim_{x}^{\mathbb{C}}Z_{j}(Q)=d-j  for every  j = 1 , … , d − r j=1,\ldots,d-r .
As a consequence, we have  dim Q , ( m ) ℝ ( x ) ≤ ( d − 1 , d − 2 , … , r ) \dim^{\mathbb{R}}_{Q,(m)}(x)\leq(d-1,d-2,\ldots,r) .
Therefore we may now apply  [ 56 , Proposition 7.1]  with  j = d − r j=d-r  and sum over all  ( d − r ) (d-r) -tuples  τ \tau  with  τ j ≤ d − j \tau_{j}\leq d-j  for all  j ∈ [ d − r ] j\in[d-r]  to get the desired bound.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 A.2.  Proof of  Theorem   3.3 

 
 We deduce  Theorem   3.3  from  [ 56 , Theorem 3.2] . The statement of that theorem involves the quantities  Δ i ​ ( V ) \Delta_{i}(V)  and  i V ​ ( A ) i_{V}(A) . We refer the reader to  [ 56 , Section 3]  for the definition of  i V ​ ( A ) i_{V}(A)  since our proof does not use any property of  i V ​ ( A ) i_{V}(A)  other than that it is an integer between 0 and  d − dim V d-\dim V . 
 
 
 Let  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  be an irreducible variety. For every index  0 ≤ i ≤ d − dim V 0\leq i\leq d-\dim V , write 
 

 
 
 Δ i ​ ( V ) = max ⁡ ( deg ⁡ V δ i + 1 ( V ) ⋯ δ d − dim V ( V ) , 1 ) . \Delta_{i}(V)=\max\left(\frac{\deg V}{\delta_{i+1}(V)\cdots\delta_{d-\dim V}(V)},1\right). 
 
 
 Note that in particular, we have  Δ d − dim V ​ ( V ) = deg ⁡ V \Delta_{d-\dim V}(V)=\deg V , and so the middle term in  Proposition   3.2  can be rewritten as  Δ d − dim V \Delta_{d-\dim V} .
We can extend  Proposition   3.2  as follows. 
 
 
 Proposition A.2 . 
 
 For any irreducible variety  V ⊆ ℂ d V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}  and any index  0 ≤ i ≤ d − dim V 0\leq i\leq d-\dim V , 
 

 
 
 ∏ j = 1 i δ j ( V ) ≲ d Δ i ( V ) ≤ ∏ j = 1 i δ j ( V ) . \prod_{j=1}^{i}\delta_{j}(V)\lesssim_{d}\Delta_{i}(V)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{i}\delta_{j}(V). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  Proposition   3.2 , 
 

 
 
 Δ i ( V ) ≥ deg ⁡ V ∏ j = i + 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) ≳ d ∏ j = 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) ∏ j = i + 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) = ∏ j = 1 i δ j ( V ) . \Delta_{i}(V)\geq\frac{\deg V}{\prod_{j=i+1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}\gtrsim_{d}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}{\prod_{j=i+1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}=\prod_{j=1}^{i}\delta_{j}(V). 
 
 
 For the other direction, it clearly holds if  Δ i ​ ( V ) = 1 \Delta_{i}(V)=1 .
Otherwise, by  Proposition   3.2 , 
 

 
 
 Δ i ​ ( V ) = deg ⁡ V ∏ j = i + 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) ≤ ∏ j = 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) ∏ j = i + 1 d − dim V δ j ​ ( V ) = ∏ j = 1 i δ j ​ ( V ) . ∎ \Delta_{i}(V)=\frac{\deg V}{\prod_{j=i+1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}\leq\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}{\prod_{j=i+1}^{d-\dim V}\delta_{j}(V)}=\prod_{j=1}^{i}\delta_{j}(V).\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   3.3 . 
 
 By directly applying  [ 56 , Theorem 3.2] , it remains to show that 
 

 
 
 | P | A d − i V ​ ( A ) ​ Δ i V ​ ( A ) ​ ( V ) ≲ d | P | A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i δ j ​ ( V ) . \frac{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}{A^{d-i_{V}(A)}\Delta_{i_{V}(A)}(V)}\lesssim_{d}\frac{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}{A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\delta_{j}(V)}. 
 
 
 Since  Δ i V ​ ( A ) ​ ( V ) ≳ ∏ j = 1 i V ​ ( A ) d ⁡ δ j ​ ( V ) \Delta_{i_{V}(A)}(V)\gtrsim_{d}\prod_{j=1}^{i_{V}(A)}\delta_{j}(V)  by  Proposition   A.2 , it suffices to show that 
 

 
 
 A d − i V ​ ( A ) ​ ∏ j = 1 i V ​ ( A ) δ j ​ ( V ) ≥ A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i δ j ​ ( V ) . A^{d-i_{V}(A)}\prod_{j=1}^{i_{V}(A)}\delta_{j}(V)\geq A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\delta_{j}(V). 
 
 
 However, by the definition of  i i  and the fact that  δ 1 ​ ( V ) ≤ ⋯ ≤ δ d − dim V ​ ( V ) \delta_{1}(V)\leq\cdots\leq\delta_{d-\dim V}(V) , we know that 
 

 
 
 A i ​ ∏ j = 1 d − i δ j ​ ( V ) = min dim V ≤ ℓ ≤ d ⁡ { A ℓ ​ ∏ j = 1 d − ℓ δ j ​ ( V ) } . A^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{d-i}\delta_{j}(V)=\min_{\dim V\leq\ell\leq d}\left\{A^{\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{d-\ell}\delta_{j}(V)\right\}. 
 
 
 Therefore the inequality follows.
∎
